## 2026-10-23 · #90 American Barn Owl — *Tyto furcata* v3

### ABO-BC-SPECIES-2026
- Title: Species Summary — *Tyto furcata*, American Barn Owl
- Institution: Government of British Columbia
- Year/access: n.d.; accessed 2026-10-23
- URL: https://a100.gov.bc.ca/pub/eswp/speciesSummary.do?id=14636
- Type: government species account, citing Bloom 1978 and Colvin 1984
- Species/stage: *Tyto furcata*, adults, sex differences
- Actual content read: adults 30–37 cm; females reported larger/heavier (569 g versus 475 g), darker and more heavily speckled; variability makes some individuals difficult to sex.
- Location: ADULTS/species summary; no page number.
- Limits/confidence: secondary synthesis, population and individual variation; body mass cannot be converted directly to image height. High confidence for direction, moderate for design magnitude.
- Decision: female remains fuller/darker/more spotted, but the v2 exaggerated perspective scale is reduced.

### ABO-MONTANA-MARTI-1992
- Title: American Barn Owl — Montana Field Guide
- Institution/source: Montana Natural Heritage Program / Montana Fish, Wildlife & Parks; morphology attributed to Marti 1992
- Year/access: current online account; accessed 2026-10-23
- URL: https://fieldguide.mt.gov/speciesDetail.aspx?elcode=ABNSA01030
- Type: government field guide / secondary account
- Species/stage: *Tyto furcata*, adult male/female
- Actual content read: females average 34–40 cm and males 32–38 cm; female breast slightly darker and sometimes heavily spotted.
- Location: Identification; no page number.
- Limits/confidence: overlapping ranges and compiled citation; does not define an individual fixed ratio. High confidence for overlap and qualitative direction.
- Decision: constrain display difference to modest, not universal.

### ABO-MARTI-SEXAGE
- Title: Sex and Age Dimorphism in the Barn Owl and a Test of Mate Choice
- Author: Carl D. Marti
- Venue/year: The Auk; original paper record, year as indexed in source record
- URL: https://digitalcommons.usf.edu/cgi/viewcontent.cgi?article=22040&context=auk
- Type: primary morphometric study
- Species/stage: barn owl adults, sex and age
- Actual content read from accessible record: females significantly larger in measured characters except wing chord and tail length; males have lower wing loading.
- Location: abstract/summary record; exact table values not extracted in this run.
- Limits/confidence: taxonomic treatment and population context require care; supports direction, not the exact 8–12% visual target. Moderate confidence for broad decision.

Cycle boundary: new/reused/corrected source IDs 3/1/0. Existing v2 actual image and status reused. v3 changes only the right adult female's display scale; adult plumage, juvenile stage and support remain invariant. Exact pair ratio, juvenile age, toe geometry and feather sequence remain unknown; `scientifically_verified=false`. ### TKBO-EBIRD-ID-2026
- Title/institution: Tawny-browed Owl, Cornell Lab of Ornithology / eBird
- URL: https://ebird.org/species/tabowl1
- Accessed: 2026-10-23
- Type: institutional identification page and Macaulay media index
- Evidence read: large brown owl with dark eyes, distinctive pale facial semicircles; humid/open woodland including Araucaria forest.
- Limits/design use: adult identification and habitat only; no exact juvenile age or sex measurements.

### TKBO-ECOREG-JUV-2026
- Title/source: *Pulsatrix koeniswaldiana*, EcoRegistros species account
- URL: https://www.ecoregistros.org/sheet/Pulsatrix-koeniswaldiana&idlugar=109873
- Accessed: 2026-10-23
- Type: regional observation/photo synthesis
- Evidence read: juveniles are mostly white or creamy with a dark mask-like facial disc and acquire adult appearance gradually.
- Limits/design use: secondary synthesis without continuous feather measurements; supports the pale dependent-juvenile direction, not exact day/wing length.

### TKBO-RSD-2018
- Title: Reverse Size Dimorphism Estimated by an Improved Method in Eight Species of Neotropical Owls
- Access: indexed research record read 2026-10-23
- URL: https://www.researchgate.net/publication/322258615_Reverse_Size_Dimorphism_Estimated_by_an_Improved_Method_in_Eight_Species_of_Neotropical_Owls
- Type: morphometric study record
- Evidence read: body mass is the more dimorphic metric; *P. koeniswaldiana* total-length dimorphism is near zero.
- Limits/design use: available record is insufficient for a painted fixed ratio; adults remain near-equal display scale.

Cycle boundary: v2 showed a central juvenile with adult-length layered remiges, regular white wing spots and a long tail. v3 changes only the juvenile to creamy-white/pale-buff mesoptile, dark mask, softer incomplete breast band, short rounded developing wings and no exposed long tail, while preserving both adults, labels, forest and two-foot branch support. Exact age, continuous feather sequence, flight ability and quantified sex ratio remain unknown; scientifically_verified=false.

Cycle boundary: v2 adults and habitat were retained. The central juvenile's long dark layered remiges and exposed tail were inconsistent with its heavily downy depiction. v3 changes only that bird to short rounded down-covered wing buds with no long exposed remiges or tail, while preserving two-foot support, adults, labels and environment. Exact juvenile age, detailed molt sequence, flight ability and quantitative sex ratio remain unknown; scientifically_verified=false.

## ELE-AKATANI2011-JRR

- **Title:** Breeding Ecology and Habitat Use of the Daito Scops Owl (*Otus elegans interpositus*) on an Oceanic Island
- **Authors:** Kana Akatani, Taro Matsuo, Masaoki Takagi
- **Year:** 2011
- **Source type:** peer-reviewed field study; full text read
- **DOI / URL:** https://doi.org/10.3356/JRR-10-82.1 ; full-text mirror read at https://artefacts-discovery.researcher.life/full_text_files/DA-2/b0/b08a46ba4b3b3b479b767cf6cfbffccb/full_text/QY9j5MIU0LPmlYpR1bcjFQzTRKFCueUozDf963dNwcM=.pdf
- **Accessed:** 2026-10-24
- **Species / stage:** *Otus elegans interpositus*; breeding adults, nest sites and nestling provisioning; no calibrated juvenile plumage sequence
- **Read location:** printed pp.315–319 (PDF pp.1–5), especially abstract, study-area/methods, nest-characteristics and diet sections
- **Verified facts:** Most recorded nests were cavities in introduced *Casuarina* (n=30); two were vertical hollows in native fan palm. The study area is a fragmented Minami-daito landscape dominated by sugar cane and forest shelterbelts; nest and habitat variables were measured directly. Nestling diet was recorded from 25 nests.
- **Design support:** Supports a Casuarina cavity with shelterbelt/agricultural-edge context for the Minami-daito concept. Does not support an exact juvenile age, feather length, sex ratio or a decorative nest substrate.
- **Limitations / confidence:** High confidence for sampled nest-site and habitat facts; no direct quantitative plumage calibration extracted. Do not infer all *O. elegans* populations use introduced Casuarina.
- **Linked artifacts:** `owl-pet/research/species/86-otus-elegans.md`; `owl-pet/production/runs/2026-10-24T0327+0800-cycle-3.md`; `owl-pet/design/concepts/86-otus-elegans-v3.png`


### RUT-PEREGRINE-2026
- Title: Madagascar Scops-owl
- Author/Institution: The Peregrine Fund / World Center for Birds of Prey
- Year/updated: accessed 2026-10-24
- URL: https://peregrinefund.org/explore-raptors-species/owls/madagascar-scops-owl
- Source type: raptor-research institution species account; full page read
- Species/stage: *Otus rutilus*; adults, habitat, breeding/young evidence limits
- Key facts: three color morphs (rufous, brown, gray), generally pale facial disc; rainforest, secondary forest and parks; daytime dense-foliage roost. Breeding and nestling development remain poorly known.
- Section: “Where They Live”, “What They Do”, “Nest, Eggs, and Young” (lines 43–59 in retrieved page)
- Limits/confidence: secondary institutional synthesis citing Birds of the World; explicitly does not establish juvenile feather sequence or a fixed sex ratio.
- Design decision: supports retaining pale discs/rufous-brown morph and forest context; supports marking juvenile age and sequence unknown. Does not support mechanically repeated ventral chevrons.
- Related: species #108; `design/concepts/108-otus-rutilus-v2.png`; `production/runs/2026-10-24T0429+0800-cycle-4.md`.

### LEU-OWLPAGES-2020
- Title: Northern White-faced Owl — *Ptilopsis leucotis*
- Author/Institution: Deane Lewis, The Owl Pages
- Year/updated: 2020-10-25; accessed 2026-10-24
- URL: https://www.owlpages.com/owls/species.php?s=1160
- Source type: specialist species synthesis citing major owl monographs; full page read
- Species/stage: *Ptilopsis leucotis*; adult soft parts, legs/feet, breeding and young
- Read location: Description and Breeding, lines 63–76 in retrieved page
- Verified facts: tarsi feathered to the basal half of the toes; distal toes dusky-brown with blackish claws. Young appear fully feathered around day 27 and may climb out; flight reported by days 30–32.
- Limits/confidence: secondary synthesis, not a specimen measurement or continuous photo series. Exact featherlet count, individual toe coverage, pictured juvenile age and fixed sex ratio remain unknown. Confidence: medium-high for descriptive direction.
- Design decision: adult proximal toe featherlets may reach roughly halfway; distal toes and claws must remain bare. Juvenile is preserved without numeric-age assignment.
- Related: species #109; `design/concepts/109-ptilopsis-leucotis-v8.png`; `production/runs/2026-10-24T0530+0800-cycle-5.md`.

### BSO-ENRIQUEZ-2010

- **Title:** The ‘Near Threatened’ Bearded Screech-owl *Megascops barbarus*: diet pattern and trophic assessment using δ13C and δ15N stable-isotopes
- **Authors / institution:** P. L. Enríquez et al.; *Bird Conservation International*
- **Year / publication:** 2010, 20(1), pp. 25–33
- **Stable URL / DOI:** https://doi.org/10.1017/S095927090999013X
- **Accessed:** 2026-10-24
- **Source type:** peer-reviewed research article
- **Species / stage:** *Megascops barbarus*; adults sampled for feathers/diet analysis (not a juvenile-plumage study)
- **Read section / valuable fact:** Abstract and introductory habitat description; 24 feather samples support a mainly insectivorous trophic signal, and the species is associated with humid montane/cloud and pine–oak forest around 1,800–2,500 m.
- **Limitations / confidence:** High confidence for habitat and diet context; no support for exact juvenile age, sex ratio, or covert geometry. It cannot justify a literal juvenile feather map.
- **Design / review decision:** supports retaining humid highland pine–oak context only. Juvenile texture correction continues to rely on the project’s field-photo sources `BSO-EISERMANN-FIELD` and `BSO-CAYAYA-FIELD`, with uncertainty preserved.
- **Links:** [species record](../knowledge/species/110-megascops-barbarus.md) · [v3](../design/concepts/110-megascops-barbarus-v3.png) · [cycle record](../production/runs/2026-10-24T0828+0800-cycle-6.md)

### BSO-IUCN-2020

- **Title:** *Megascops barbarus* — Bearded Screech-owl
- **Author / institution:** BirdLife International / IUCN Red List
- **Year / accessed:** assessment 2020; accessed 2026-10-24
- **URL:** https://doi.org/10.2305/IUCN.UK.2020-3.RLTS.T22688807A180130085.en
- **Source type:** authoritative conservation assessment synthesizing published natural-history sources
- **Species / stage:** *Megascops barbarus*; nesting and approximately three-week nestling
- **Read location / facts:** Habitat and Ecology: montane evergreen and humid pine–oak forest at 1,800–2,500 m; the known June 2001 nest was a natural cavity 2.45 m above ground in a living *Quercus laurina*, with an adult red-morph female brooding one grey-morph nestling estimated at three weeks.
- **Limitations / confidence:** The assessment does not publish a continuous photographic feather sequence or literal covert map. “Grey morph” and estimated age do not justify neat repeated arcs, exact feather counts, flight ability or a fixed sex-size ratio. Confidence high for the cited nest context, medium for using the brief stage description to bound art.
- **Design decision:** v5 may depict one grey-brown approximately three-week nestling/older juvenile in an oak-cavity/pine–oak setting, with soft irregular developing coverts and no mechanical scallop rows; any formed remiges must remain modest and stage-bounded.
- **Related:** #110; `owl-pet/knowledge/species/110-megascops-barbarus.md`; `owl-pet/production/runs/2026-10-24T1431+0800-cycle-2.md`.

### RBO-2026-10-24-REUSE-NOTE
Cycle2 re-read RBO-GREENEY-2003 full text pp.67–68 and reused RBO-SCHERZINGER-1986 p.41. Greeney directly supports a yellow-cream down juvenile roosting beside an adult at the base of tree-fern leaves about 4 m high in disturbed primary forest with Chusquea bamboo, but gives no iris colour, age or wing sequence. Scherzinger's adult-head comparison explicitly describes *Strix albitarsis* as having a dark eye in a dark facial field and warns of an erroneous historical drawing. Current secondary pages/search snippets variously say orange/yellow; because their underlying specimen/sample and lighting are not resolved in the accessible text, this cycle treats conspicuous amber as unsupported and uses a very dark brown iris without claiming universal calibrated colour. Stable source IDs reused: RBO-GREENEY-2003, RBO-SCHERZINGER-1986. Accessed 2026-10-24. This is a correction note, not a new source entry.


### SWFO-SABISABI-2014
- **Title / institution:** Sabi Sabi Wild Facts: Southern White Faced Scops Owl / Sabi Sabi Private Game Reserve
- **Published / accessed:** 2014-12-16 / 2026-10-25
- **URL:** https://www.sabisabi.com/discover/wild-facts/white-faced-scops-owl
- **Source type:** field-site institutional species account; full page read
- **Species / stage:** *Ptilopsis granti*; adults, juveniles and fledging
- **Read location / facts:** lines 220–235. Juveniles are described with greyish facial plumage and yellow eyes; young begin flying at roughly 33 days. The account describes dry woodland, thornveld and riverine forest, and use of old stick nests or natural tree holes.
- **Limitations / confidence:** Secondary field account without sample size, measured feather sequence or a dated juvenile photo series. It cannot establish exact age, covert geometry, toe-feather count or fixed sex ratio. Confidence medium for broad stage and habitat direction.
- **Design decision:** Supports a grey-faced, yellow-grey-eyed older juvenile in dry woodland with formed but not adult-like wings; exact age remains unknown and mechanical repeated covert arcs are not evidence-supported.
- **Related:** #105; `owl-pet/knowledge/species/105-ptilopsis-granti.md`; `owl-pet/production/runs/2026-10-25T0128+0800-cycle-4.md`.


### MARS-HERZOG-2009
- **Title:** Vocalizations, Distribution, and Ecology of the Cloud-Forest Screech Owl (*Megascops marshalli*)
- **Authors:** Sebastian K. Herzog et al.
- **Year / journal:** 2009, *The Wilson Journal of Ornithology* 121(2):240–252
- **DOI / URL:** https://doi.org/10.1676/08-041.1 ; author full text read at https://www.researchgate.net/publication/224825121_Vocalizations_Distribution_and_Ecology_of_the_Cloud-Forest_Screech_Owl_Megascops_marshalli
- **Accessed:** 2026-10-25
- **Source type:** peer-reviewed field, specimen-comparison and vocalization study; full text read
- **Species / stage:** *M. marshalli* adults/specimens and habitat; no juvenile developmental series
- **Read location / facts:** abstract pp.240–241; specimen comparison and Fig.2 around pp.243–244. Confirms a Bolivian specimen by direct comparison with Peruvian material; three illustrated males show dorsal geographic/individual variation, with the Bolivian crown/nape darker than Peruvian paratypes. Preferred habitat is pristine to slightly disturbed wet montane forest with dense understory and abundant epiphytes, recorded at 1,550–2,580 m.
- **Limitations / confidence:** Male specimen comparison does not define female differences, juvenile stages, a universal crown tone or exact hind-crown feather geometry. High confidence for examined specimens and habitat; not a continuous development study.
- **Design decision:** Use mossy epiphyte-rich cloud forest and allow restrained crown/nape variation. Do not turn one specimen's pale hind-crown feathers into a universal broad cap or infer a fixed sex ratio.
- **Related:** #128; `owl-pet/knowledge/species/128-megascops-marshalli.md`; `owl-pet/production/runs/2026-10-25T0230+0800-cycle-5.md`.

### SLSO-REUSE-2026-10-25
- **Accessed:** 2026-10-25
- **Species/stage:** *Otus manadensis*, adults and older juvenile.
- **Reused source IDs:** `SLSO-EBIRD-JUV-2026`, `SLSO-FWS-TAXONOMY-2026`, `SLSO-MANAHAMPI-2012`, `SLSO-MIK2013-194`.
- **Freshly read evidence:** Cornell Lab/eBird current account (https://ebird.org/species/susowl1) supports yellow-eyed brown adults with ear tufts and juvenile heavier barring on crown/back in forest/edge; USFWS current profile (https://www.fws.gov/species/sulawesi-scops-owl-otus-manadensis) supports the current scientific combination only.
- **Limits:** no exact juvenile age, continuous feather sequence, flight ability, literal feather geometry, fixed sex ratio or toe-feather count. Uploaded Mikkola file was unavailable this invocation, so the prior p.194 index entry is reused without a new page-reading claim.
- **Design decision:** irregular diffuse juvenile ventral texture; no mechanical scallop rows or adult-length remiges. Related: [#117 v8](../design/concepts/117-otus-manadensis-v8.png), [cycle record](../production/runs/2026-10-25T0431+0800-cycle-1.md).


### MINBOO-REUSE-2026-10-25
- **Accessed:** 2026-10-25
- **Species/stage:** *Ninox spilocephala*, adults and age-unknown older immature.
- **Reused source IDs:** `LZBO-RASMUSSEN-2012-LEAD`, `MINBOO-EBIRD-ID-2026`, `MINBOO-AVIBASE-TAXON-2026`.
- **Freshly read evidence:** Cornell Lab/eBird current account, https://ebird.org/species/minboo1, directly supports a fairly small lowland-forest owl with dark-brown head/upperparts, wing speckles, reddish-brown chest, white belly with thick reddish-brown streaking and yellowish eyes; it explicitly distinguishes the species from Chocolate Boobook by smaller size and absence of white on the chest. Rasmussen et al. (2012) remains the primary taxonomy/morphology source already indexed for the *N. philippensis* complex and the old-name mapping.
- **Limits:** no exact immature age, continuous wing/tail sequence, soft-part calibration, quantified sex ratio, nest structure or locomotor milestone. BirdLife's current dynamic factsheet was opened but exposed no substantive species fields in the retrieved page, so it is not counted as a new verified evidence entry.
- **Design decision:** v4 keeps adults near-equal, enforces reddish-brown upper chest without a white patch, places white only on the streaked lower belly, and uses a supported older immature with compact wings/tail. Related: [#144 v4](../design/concepts/144-ninox-spilocephala-v4.png), [cycle record](../production/runs/2026-10-25T0532+0800-cycle-2.md).

## 2026-10-25 · #156 Pel's Fishing Owl — *Scotopelia peli* v2

- **PFO-BIRDLIFE-DATAZONE-2026** — BirdLife International Data Zone, [Pel's Fishing-owl species factsheet](https://datazone.birdlife.org/species/factsheet/pels-fishing-owl-scotopelia-peli), current page accessed 2026-10-25; authoritative conservation/taxonomic factsheet. Read facts: accepted species *Scotopelia peli*, resident African range and forest/wetland association. The dynamic page exposed no measured juvenile sequence or sex dimensions in the retrieved text. Confidence high for identity/range, low for developmental calibration.
- **Reused:** `PFO-BLSA-REDLIST-2025`, `PFO-FWS-TAX-2026`, `PFO-OWLPAGES-2020`, `PFO-OUZMAN-JUV`. BirdLife South Africa supports orange-rufous adults, dark eyes, visually similar sexes with only a subtle female size direction, bare fishing-adapted tarsi/toes, natural cavities, and prolonged pale juvenile down; Owl Pages supports ginger-rufous adults, bare straw-coloured lower legs/toes and cavity nesting.
- **Evidence boundary:** exact juvenile age, day-by-day feather-tract sequence, locomotor milestone, fish-gripping spicule geometry and quantitative sex ratio remain unknown. Uploaded Mikkola guide was not accessible in this execution, so no new page claim is made.
- **Design / review:** v2 keeps adults near-equal, dark-eyed, round-headed and ear-tuft-free with bare fishing feet. First generated juvenile had adult-like barred remiges; one targeted edit replaced them with short rounded down-covered wings and a concealed tail. Related image: `design/concepts/156-scotopelia-peli-v2.png`; run: `production/runs/2026-10-25T1030+0800-cycle-6-recovery.md`.

### 2026-10-25 · #84 *Otus bakkamoena* v7 reuse and fresh check
- **Accessed:** 2026-10-25.
- **Reused verified IDs:** `BAK-BRRAICH-SINGH2021`, `BAK-MIK2013-150`, `ISO-EBIRD-ID-2026`, `ISO-PF-FACE-2026`, `ISO-OWLPAGES-DESC-2020`.
- **Freshly read:** Cornell Lab/eBird current account (https://ebird.org/species/insowl1) supports prominent ear tufts, dark eyes, variable grey-brown plumage, thin black facial-disc border and woodland habitat; The Peregrine Fund current account (https://peregrinefund.org/explore-raptors-species/owls/indian-scops-owl) supports tree-hole nesting and explicitly states that young are poorly known; Owl Pages (https://www.owlpages.com/owls/species.php?s=760) supports cavities, woodland/orchards and limited breeding knowledge.
- **Primary juvenile boundary:** Brraich & Singh photo 70, Indian BIRDS 17(2), p.56, remains the actual-read source for one supported juvenile: grey horizontal barring, no ear tufts, blue-grey eyes, grey bill, underdeveloped wings and supported feet. This is one brood/observation and is not generalized into a universal age sequence.
- **Design decision:** v7 is a full evidence-bounded rebuild rather than another unsupported eye-ring edit: adults have dark hazel-brown eyes within one continuous pale grey-buff disc and a thin dark outer rim; juvenile has blue-grey eyes and grey bill, short soft wings, short concealed tail and secure cavity-floor support. Adults shown near-equal with only subtle female fullness; no quantified sex ratio claim.
- **Unknown:** exact juvenile age, continuous feather sequence, flight ability, universal juvenile eye colour, toe-feather geometry and quantified sexual dimorphism. Uploaded Mikkola guide was unavailable this invocation, so the already indexed printed pp.150–151 are reused without a new page-reading claim.

## 2026-10-26 #21 *Megascops choliba* v4 evidence increment

Freshly opened/reused 2026-10-26:
- `TSO-ACTA-NESTLING-2017`: Guilherme & de Souza, original Acta Amazonica paper and PDF; monitored a southwestern Amazon nest from hatching to departure. Exact pin length, continuous feather sequence, reference subspecies and universal cavity geometry remain unknown; `scientifically_verified=false`.

## 2026-10-26 · #58 *Ketupa coromanda* v2 evidence increment

### DEO-REDDY-RAMACHANDRAN-2023
- Title: Breeding of the Dusky Eagle Owl *Ketupa coromanda* in Telangana, and its status in southern India
- Authors/institution: Sriram Reddy & Gopalakrishna Ramachandran; Indian BIRDS 19(2):44–47
- Year/publication: 2023; accessed 2026-10-26
- Original URL: https://indianbirds.in/pdfs/IB_19_2_Reddy_Ramachandran_DuskyEagleOwl.pdf
- Source type: primary species-specific field observation with photographs
- Species/stage: *Ketupa coromanda* adults, breeding pair and approximately 8–10-week juvenile
- Evidence actually read: p.44 records paired adults, with the female identified as slightly larger after mating behaviour; a large dry-stick nest c.20 m high in a tall tree beside reserve forest. p.45 reports a camouflaged juvenile moving among neighbouring branches on 13 March 2021, estimated at 8–10 weeks by appearance and behaviour; parents watched from a distance. The site adjoined a large waterbody, wooded forest and rice cultivation.
- Page/figure: pp.44–45; photographs 71–73; lines 17–60 and 65–102 in extracted PDF.
- Limits/confidence: two opportunistic locality records; juvenile age is a field estimate, not a measured growth series; the female-size inference comes from one behaviorally sexed pair. High for the observed stage/context, medium for generalizing age or dimorphism.
- Design/review use: supported branch-moving 8–10-week juvenile beside a high reused stick nest; only subtle female fullness; do not infer an exact remex sequence or universal size ratio.
- Linked record/version/run: `knowledge/species/58-ketupa-coromanda.md`; `design/concepts/58-ketupa-coromanda-v2.png`; `production/runs/2026-10-26T2225+0800-cycle-2.md`.

### Reused sources and design boundary
- Reused `DEO-AVIBASE-2026`, `DEO-BIRDLIFE-2026`, `DEO-ADW-2026`, and `DEO-JUV-OBS-2026`.
- Freshly read Peregrine Fund synthesis supports yellow eyes, long ear tufts, lowland riparian/woodland habitat, old high stick nests in tree forks and major uncertainty in breeding timing.
- Source fact versus inference: the primary paper supports an 8–10-week branch-moving youngster but not exact feather geometry. Wings stay short and down-softened; scientific calibration remains pending.


## 2026-10-26 · #59 *Ketupa zeylonensis* v2 evidence increment

### BFOW-BINDU-BALAKRISHNAN-2015
- Title/authors: T. N. Bindu & Peroth Balakrishnan, “Observations on the Breeding of the Brown Fish-Owl *Ketupa zeylonensis* in Kerala, Southern India,” *Journal of the Bombay Natural History Society* 112(2):97–98 (2015).
- Stable URL/DOI: https://doi.org/10.17087/jbnhs/2015/v112i2/104938
- Accessed: 2026-10-26; source type: primary nine-year breeding observation; article metadata plus accessible full-text sections/table actually read.
- Evidence: nine nesting attempts; mean incubation 34.8±1.92 d (32–37 d, n=5); nestling period 43–49 d (mean 46±3 d, n=3); seven of eleven eggs fledged. The repeatedly used site indicates probable site fidelity but birds were unmarked. The paper notes large trees and sites close to water.
- Limits/confidence: long monitoring but one repeatedly used locality/site and unmarked adults; it does not provide a day-by-day feather tract. High for observed timing, medium for universalizing nest choice or plumage.
- Design use: v2 uses a late-nestling/near-fledging stage bounded by the 43–49 d nestling period, without inventing a precise remex sequence.
- Linked record/version/run: `knowledge/species/59-ketupa-zeylonensis.md`; `design/concepts/59-ketupa-zeylonensis-v2.png`; `production/runs/2026-10-26T2218+0800-cycle-3.md`.

### Reused evidence and v1 review
- Reused the actually read Indian BIRDS 8(6) breeding account: 15–20 d chicks and two nestlings that left 23/25 Apr 2012; fledglings resembled adults and were nearly adult-sized but remained dependent. Reused Owl Pages/Avibase adult and taxonomy records.
- v1 was actually opened: composition/support passed, but adult irises read amber-orange rather than clear yellow, and juvenile wing/tail were too mature. v2 corrects both.

- Title/author/institution: Chen-ning Wang (汪辰寧), *Diet and activity pattern of Tawny fish owl (Ketupa flavipes) nestlings in Wuling* / 武陵地區黃魚鴞育雛食性及活動模式, M.Sc. thesis, National Pingtung University of Science and Technology, Institute of Wildlife Conservation, 2013, 39 pp.; advisor Yuan-Hsun Sun.

- Accessed: 2026-10-26; thesis metadata, bilingual abstract, contents and bibliography actually read.

### PFO-BOTSWANAPOST-2022 — BotswanaPost “Pel’s Owl in Botswana” brochure

- **Stable ID:** `PFO-BOTSWANAPOST-2022`
- **Title:** Pel’s Owl in Botswana
- **Author / institution:** BotswanaPost Philatelic Bureau; acknowledgements include Botswana Ministry of Environment, Natural Resources Conservation and Tourism, Ian White (BirdLife Botswana), Dr Tim and June Liversedge
- **Year / date:** 2022-08-31
- **URL:** https://philately.botswanapost.post/booklet/PelsFishingOwl%20brochure.pdf
- **Accessed:** 2026-10-27
- **Source type:** institutional two-page species brochure / regional field synthesis
- **Species / life stage:** *Scotopelia peli*; adults, nest and chick/fledging
- **Pages / sections:** p. 2, “Pel’s Owl in Botswana” (PDF page index 1; text lines 96–158)
- **Verified facts:** dense riverine forest around permanent water in Botswana; adults ginger-brown to russet with black spotting, rounded head and inky-black eyes; ancient indigenous trees supply cavities and waterside fishing perches; no constructed nest, eggs placed in a large-tree hollow; female incubates while male provisions; surviving chick fledges at about 70 days.
- **Design support:** adults should be round-headed, black-eyed and rufous in shaded riverine woodland; nest context may use a natural old-tree cavity without invented nest material; an older supported juvenile is safer than an undocumented natal chick.
- **Limitations / conflicts:** regional summary, not a primary growth study; brochure gives no staged juvenile-plumage measurements and does not justify exact feather-length ratios. Use its ~70-day fledging statement only to bound locomotor ability, not to assign the pictured juvenile an exact age.
- **Confidence:** medium-high for regional habitat, adult identity and cavity nesting; medium for life-history summary.
- **Linked records:** `research/species/63-scotopelia-peli.md`; `design/concepts/63-scotopelia-peli-v2.png`; `production/runs/2026-10-27T0034+0800-cycle-1.md`
- **Fact / inference boundary:** source facts are listed above; choosing an age-unknown branch-perched older juvenile with short down-softened developing wings is a conservative design inference constrained by the lack of a species-specific plumage series.

- **Species / life stage:** *Tyto glaucops* adults; breeding habitat and sexual mass direction
- **Sections / lines read:** “Endemic Bird of the Day: Ashy-faced Owl,” lines 10–19 and photo notes line 33
- **Verified facts:** Hispaniola endemic; adult has an ash-gray heart-shaped face, black eyes and buff underparts, darker than the similar Barn Owl; recorded habitats are broad, with a stated preference for dense wooded areas; natural and artificial cavity nesting; females reported >500 g versus males 300–350 g.
- **Design support:** use a genuinely gray, dark-rimmed facial disc rather than a Barn Owl-white face; keep black eyes and buff/mottled underparts; convey female mass direction through fuller body volume only, not an invented standing-height ratio.
- **Limitations / conflicts:** educational synthesis rather than a primary morphometric paper; habitat preference wording differs from the Peregrine Fund profile, which emphasizes lightly wooded/open settings. Treat the species as habitat-flexible and choose a plausible Hispaniolan palm/woodland edge rather than claiming a universal setting. The page supplies no juvenile feather chronology.
- **Confidence:** medium-high for adult identification and sex mass direction; medium for generalized habitat preference.
- **Linked records:** `knowledge/species/64-tyto-glaucops.md`; `design/concepts/64-tyto-glaucops-v2.png`; `production/runs/2026-10-27T0034+0800-cycle-2.md`
- **Fact / inference boundary:** adult traits and relative mass are source facts; visual body-volume differences and habitat staging are conservative design inferences.


### MRO-THORSTROM-NEST-1996 — First nesting record of Madagascar Red Owl

- **Stable ID:** `MRO-THORSTROM-NEST-1996`
- **Title:** Preliminary study and first nesting record of the Madagascar Red Owl, *Tyto soumagnei*
- **Author / institution:** Russell Thorstrom and Karen E. Rabarisoa; The Peregrine Fund / WWF Madagascar Project
- **Year:** 1996 (Working Group on Birds in the Madagascar Region Newsletter 6(1):9–12)
- **URL:** https://assets.peregrinefund.org/docs/pdf/research-library/1997/1997-Thorstrom-red-owl-nest.pdf
- **Accessed:** 2026-10-27
- **Source type:** primary field research / first documented nest; six-page scanned PDF
- **Species / life stage:** *Tyto soumagnei* adults and two young, nest to fledging
- **Pages / figures read:** newsletter pp. 9–11 (PDF pp. 1–3), especially p. 9 nest setting and age estimate, p. 10–11 fledging account; Fig. 1 study habitat; Fig. 2 adult at cavity; Fig. 3 wing of fledged young
- **Verified facts:** nest was a natural cavity in a partly dead *Weinmannia* about 23 m high in primary humid rainforest near Ambanizana on the Masoala Peninsula; two young were estimated 6–7 weeks old when found; both left the cavity and fledged at about 10–11 weeks; fledged young were fully plumaged and generally more brown than adults, with reddish-orange overcast on facial and body feathers; an adult foraging male was smaller than the female by measurements reported in the paper.
- **Design support:** a recently fledged, naturally supported juvenile can be shown fully feathered yet browner/softer than adults; use a high humid-forest cavity in an old partly dead tree and avoid a ground chick or invented natal-down stage.
- **Limitations / conflicts:** single first-known nest and two young; findings should not be generalized to every population. Exact daily feather sequence between 6 and 11 weeks was not documented. Scanned copy was read visually, so the index preserves concise facts rather than OCR-derived quotations.
- **Confidence:** high for this observed nest, fledging window and plumage direction; medium for broader species-wide generalization.
- **Linked records:** `knowledge/species/65-tyto-soumagnei.md`; `design/concepts/65-tyto-soumagnei-v2.png`; `production/runs/2026-10-27T0034+0800-cycle-3.md`
- **Fact / inference boundary:** field measurements and observations above are facts from one nest; lineup pose, subtle softness and branch placement are design inferences.


### GSO-ABSA-2019 — Australian Bird Study Association Sooty Owl profile

- **Stable ID:** `GSO-ABSA-2019`
- **Title:** Sooty Owl *Tyto tenebricosa tenebricosa* — Bird in the Hand, 2nd ed.
- **Author / institution:** J.W. Hardy; Australian Bird Study Association; reproduced with BirdLife Australia permission; HANZAB 4 basis
- **Published:** 2019-03
- **URL:** https://absa.asn.au/wp-content/uploads/2019/09/Sooty-Owl.pdf
- **Accessed:** 2026-10-27
- **Source type:** institutional banding/morphometrics profile
- **Species / stage:** nominate *T. t. tenebricosa* adults and fledged juveniles
- **Page:** single-page PDF, lines 10–33
- **Verified facts:** female wing/tail/tarsus and mass ranges exceed male ranges; juveniles fledge in nearly complete adult-like juvenile plumage with little remaining down and may be darker than adults; females often have lighter facial masks and underparts.
- **Design support:** show a clearly fuller female without converting mass to a precise standing-height ratio; use a fully feathered darker fledgling with only small residual down tufts rather than a natal chick.
- **Limitations:** southern nominate subspecies profile; does not provide a day-by-day feather sequence or full nest context.
- **Confidence:** high for listed measurements and ageing statement within the nominate subspecies.

### AMO-NSW-2024 — NSW Masked Owl threatened-species profile

- **Stable ID:** `AMO-NSW-2024`
- **Title:** Masked Owl — profile
- **Author / institution:** NSW Environment, Energy and Science / BioNet threatened species program
- **Last updated:** 2024-03-13
- **URL:** https://threatenedspecies.bionet.nsw.gov.au/profile?id=10820
- **Accessed:** 2026-10-27
- **Source type:** current state-government threatened-species profile
- **Species / life stage:** *Tyto novaehollandiae* adults; habitat and nesting
- **Sections / lines read:** Description and Habitat and ecology, lines 198–216
- **Verified facts:** dark eyes in a flat heart-shaped facial disc with a dark border; powerful feet and legs feathered to the toes; upperparts grey to dark brown with buff/rufous mottling and fine pale spots; barred wings/tail; variable pale-to-rufous underparts with dark spotting; dry eucalypt forest/woodland use, with roosting and breeding in moist eucalypt gullies in large tree hollows or sometimes caves.
- **Design support:** combine a large natural eucalypt hollow and forest-edge context with powerful fully feathered feet; keep adult colour within the named dark/brown nominate morph rather than borrowing sooty-grey *T. tenebricosa*.
- **Limitations / conflicts:** overview does not provide sex-specific dimensions or juvenile development. It explicitly notes wide colour-form variation, so it cannot justify one plumage as universal.
- **Confidence:** high for NSW identification, habitat and nesting summary.
- **Linked records:** `knowledge/species/69-tyto-novaehollandiae.md`; `design/concepts/69-tyto-novaehollandiae-v2.png`; `production/runs/2026-10-27T0034+0800-cycle-5.md`
- **Fact / inference boundary:** profile facts are listed above; choosing a dark-morph nominate family and a single hollow scene is a bounded design decision, not a species-wide universal.


### BARK-BULLOCK-2026 — Barking Owl breeding and juvenile dependence
- **Title:** Breeding and diet of a family of Barking Owls *Ninox connivens* on the mid-north coast of New South Wales
- **Author / institution:** Trevor Bullock; Australian Field Ornithology / BirdLife Australia
- **Year:** 2026 (volume 43)
- **Stable URL:** https://afo.birdlife.org.au/afo/index.php/afo/article/view/2416
- **Accessed:** 2026-10-27
- **Source type:** Primary longitudinal field observation
- **Species / stage:** *Ninox connivens*; breeding, nestling and post-fledging dependent juvenile
- **Actually read:** Article landing page and abstract
- **Page / section:** Abstract
- **Evidence facts:** One family was followed from 2023 to 2025 with juvenile independence observed into autumn 2026. Reported nestling periods were 37–39 days for the first two fledglings and 40–41 days for the third; post-fledging dependence lasted about 5–6 months.
- **Design use:** Supports depicting a formed, perching recent fledgling that remains dependent rather than treating fledging as immediate independence. It does not establish an exact plumage sequence for every age.
- **Limitations / confidence:** Single family/site and abstract-level read; useful primary development timing, but not a universal age-to-plumage map. High confidence for the reported observations, moderate transferability.
- **Related records/assets:** `owl-pet/production/runs/2026-10-27T0034+0800-cycle-6.md`; planned `owl-pet/design/concepts/71-ninox-connivens-v2.png`

- **Title:** Barking Owl, *Ninox connivens*
- **Author / institution:** Museums Victoria Collections
- **Year / publication date:** undated collection page; accessed 2026-10-27
- **Stable URL:** https://collections.museumsvictoria.com.au/species/8473

- **Actually read:** Identification and biology sections on the public page

- **Evidence facts:** Page describes pale underparts with rufous/grey-brown streaking, white-spotted wings/back, white eyebrows, yellow talons, males up to about 44 cm, females about 39 cm, permanent pairs and tree-hollow nesting. It states brown eyes.
- **Conflict / decision:** The brown-eye wording conflicts with the yellow-eye direction in Australian Museum `BARK-AM-2022` and Mikkola `WHSO-MIKKOLA-2013`. The concept plate retains yellow irises and records this page only as a conflict; no unsupported reconciliation is claimed.
- **Limitations / confidence:** Institutional overview without a cited specimen-age or iris-method context on the page; moderate confidence for general morphology, low confidence for overriding the iris consensus.

### CLA-PONCE-GONZALEZ-2025 — Striped Owl nest and chick development in Panama
- **Title:** Notas sobre la anidación del Búho Listado (*Asio clamator*) en el Cerro San Cristóbal, Chiriquí, Panamá
- **Authors / institution:** Marcos Ponce and Edgar González; Universidad Autónoma de Chiriquí / Vida Natural
- **Year:** 2025, 2(2)
- **DOI / stable URL:** https://doi.org/10.59722/rcvn.v2i2.807
- **Accessed:** 2026-10-27
- **Source type:** Primary field observation, periodic nest visits
- **Species / stage:** *Asio clamator*; 2–3-week nestlings and approximately 5-week brancher/fledgling
- **Actually read:** Full-text extract, pp. 37–43, including methods, results, discussion and figure captions
- **Evidence facts:** A rudimentary ground nest lay at a tree base in grass and shrubs, lined with feathers, straw and leaves. At discovery, 2–3-week chicks had salmon-colored body/legs, white facial down and a poorly defined facial disc. About 22 days later, one approximately 5-week chick perched 4 m high; head, underparts and legs were dull yellowish, the facial disc was defined with blackish outer-eye markings, white brow/moustache feathers were prominent, and ear tufts were defined. The authors estimate an approximately 30–40-day rearing interval and explicitly note sparse knowledge.
- **Design use:** Supports a naturally gripping approximately 5-week brancher with formed wings and defined tufts, rather than a down-only hatchling or a fully adult-plumaged juvenile; supports ground-nest grass/leaf context rather than a tree cavity.
- **Limitations / confidence:** One nest in Panama; ages are estimates, only one chick survived to the final observation, and plumage transfer across subspecies/localities is uncertain. High confidence for the reported individual and nest, moderate generalizability.
- **Related records/assets:** `owl-pet/production/runs/2026-10-27T0334+0800-cycle-1.md`; planned `owl-pet/design/concepts/76-asio-clamator-v2.png`


## STY-CADENA-2018
- **Title:** Notes on the diet, juvenile development and distribution of the Stygian Owl *Asio stygius* in Ecuador
- **Authors / institution:** Héctor Cadena-Ortíz, Jorge Bedoya, Glenda M. Pozo-Zamora, Julie Watson & Jorge Brito; Revista Ecuatoriana de Ornitología
- **Year / publication:** 2018; received 2016-12-12, accepted 2018-06-21, online 2018-09-13
- **URL / DOI:** https://doi.org/10.18272/reo.v0i3.773 ; full text https://revistas.usfq.edu.ec/index.php/reo/en/article/download/773/1244/3472
- **Accessed:** 2026-10-27
- **Source type:** Primary field study; full 12-page PDF text actually read
- **Species / stage:** *Asio stygius* in Pichincha, Ecuador; two juveniles followed for 35 days, first seen at an inferred age greater than one month
- **Pages / sections / figures:** printed pp. 20–26 (PDF pp. 1–7), especially Methods pp. 21–22, Results/Table 3/Fig. 2 pp. 23–24, Discussion pp. 25–26
- **Verified facts:** juveniles were observed perched between adults; yellow irises persisted; facial disc was black, ear tufts small, scattered body down produced a fluffy aspect, and pale feathers occurred on the rear head. Belly barring darkened over time and parallel herringbone-like adult-direction chest lines began appearing. By 20 May the juveniles rapidly changed branches and one spread its wings; authors inferred they were older than one month when first seen because 27-day chicks in another study still could not perch high. The family used an *Inga* perch at about 1.5 m in a rural site with pasture, scattered fruit trees and native vegetation remnants.
- **Evidence limitations / conflicts / confidence:** two juveniles from one Ecuador site; initial age is inferred, not hatch-dated. It does not establish a universal flight threshold, exact feather lengths, subspecies-wide plumage, or sex ratio. High confidence for the observed plumage/activity sequence at this site.
- **Design / review decision:** supports a naturally branch-perched, >1-month juvenile with yellow eyes, black face, small tufts, remaining down, barred belly and developing—not adult-identical—formed wings; supports dynamic but fully supported branch stance. Do not depict a helpless down-only chick or claim an exact fledging day.
- **Links:** [Cycle 2 run](../production/runs/2026-10-27T0334+0800-cycle-2.md) · target `design/concepts/77-asio-stygius-v2.png`

- **Title:** Observations of ground nesting, diet, and distribution of Stygian Owl (*Asio stygius siguapa*) on Isla de la Juventud, Cuba
- **Author / institution:** Seriocha Amaro-Valdés; Instituto de Ecología y Sistemática; Journal of Caribbean Ornithology 37
- **Year / publication:** 2024-03-13
- **URL / DOI:** https://doi.org/10.55431/jco.2024.37.18-26 ; https://jco.birdscaribbean.org/index.php/jco/article/view/1405
- **Accessed:** 2026-10-27
- **Source type:** Primary research note; publisher abstract and metadata actually read; full PDF retrieval failed in this run
- **Species / stage:** *Asio stygius siguapa*; adult nesting record
- **Page / section:** journal pp. 18–26; publisher abstract only
- **Verified facts:** an adult of the Cuban endemic subspecies was observed nesting on the ground on Isla de la Juventud in October 2006; the authors treat this as confirmation of ground nesting in the Cuban archipelago.
- **Evidence limitations / conflicts / confidence:** abstract-only verification; no chick morphology, nest microstructure or photographs were read. Cuban subspecies facts are not automatically transferred to the Ecuador juvenile concept.
- **Design / review decision:** use only as corroboration that ground nesting occurs within the species complex; it does not justify adding a Cuban nest or juvenile details to this Ecuador-bounded v2 plate.
- **Links:** [Cycle 2 run](../production/runs/2026-10-27T0334+0800-cycle-2.md)


## 2026-10-27 #78 *Asio capensis* v2 evidence increment

| ID | Title / author / year / URL | Source type and scope | Actually read evidence | Limits / design decision |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| MAR-ANSARA-ROSS-2008 | Tahla M. Ansara-Ross, Victor Wepener, Gerhard H. Verdoorn & Mathew J. Ross, “Sexual dimorphism of four owl species in South Africa”, *Ostrich* 79(1), 2008, DOI [10.2989/OSTRICH.2008.79.1.11.366](https://doi.org/10.2989/OSTRICH.2008.79.1.11.366); author-uploaded full text [ResearchGate](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/233710566_Sexual_dimorphism_of_four_owl_species_in_South_Africa), accessed 2026-10-27 | Primary morphometric study; Gauteng N17 road-killed intact adults, 2002–2003; *A. capensis* total n=43, sexed male n=21 / female n=11 for body mass | Methods, Results and Tables 1–2 read. Male mean mass 297.59 g vs female 329.07 g; male tail 148.8 mm vs female 156.3 mm; body mass and tail length were significant between sexes, while mean flattened wing length was nearly identical (398.1 vs 398.2 mm). No dichromatic sex distinction was found. | Roadkill regional sample with overlapping ranges; carcass total length is not artwork standing height. Supports only a modestly fuller/longer-tailed female, not a fixed height ratio or color difference. High for sampled morphometrics. |

Reused sources: MARSH-BIOEXP-2026 (actual Breeding section reread: 14–18-day chicks leave the ground depression and crawl through nearby bush before flight), MARSH-OWLPAGES-2020 (adult dark eyes, tiny tufts, plain earth-brown direction; prior verified extract), and WHSO-MIKKOLA-2013 printed pp.518–519 / PDF pp.520–521 (prior verified guide extract: buff-brown barred mesoptile, darker face/black ruff, feathered legs). Smith & Ellick-Kendrick 1964 DOI 10.1111/j.1474-919x.1964.tb03687.x was found as a bibliographic lead only; full text was not read and it is not used as design proof.

Design inference: southern African nominate concept; female only modestly fuller with a subtly longer tail; central juvenile is a low, naturally supported mobile preflight 14–18-day stage with short soft wing and concealed tail. Exact individual age, continuous feather sequence, flight threshold, subspecies geometry and universal sex ratio remain unknown. Scientifically verified: false.

Links: [species record](../knowledge/species/78-asio-capensis.md) · [run](../production/runs/2026-10-27T0334+0800-cycle-3.md) · target [v2 image](../design/concepts/78-asio-capensis-v2.png).


## 2026-10-27 #80 *Otus sunia* v2 evidence increment
SUNIA-OAK-2015 was freshly read in full through the Open Access Korea page, not only abstract/methods. Results §Clutch size, incubation and brooding period reports 13 nests, mean incubation 24.4±1.2 d, mean brooding 23.5±2.6 d and hatching asynchrony 3.5±0.7 d; Methods defines fledging as the young stably flying to the top of a tree after leaving the nest. The study covers South Korean *O. s. stictonotus*, 2011–2012, and records 46.1% woodpecker holes, 38.5% natural tree holes, 15.4% artificial boxes. This corrects the old index limitation that only Introduction/Methods/Abstract had been read. It still does not describe age-specific plumage, so Mikkola mesoptile facts remain separate and the artwork does not claim exact day-to-feather mapping.

Reused source IDs: SUNIA-OAK-2015 (fresh full-text expansion/correction), SUNIA-OWLPAGES-2020 (adult yellow eyes/dark-gray bill/white brows/gray-brown morph/cavity direction), WHSO-MIKKOLA-2013 printed pp.152–153 / PDF pp.154–155 (prior verified mesoptile: fluffy, faintly barred, markings less prominent; female about 6 g heavier). New sources 0; reused 3; corrected-scope 1. Design inference: a late cavity young near the species' mean brooding period, but no exact individual day; short rounded developing wing and natural cavity-lip support. Exact feather chronology, flight threshold, sex ratio and cross-subspecies transfer remain unknown. scientifically_verified=false.

Links: [species](../knowledge/species/80-otus-sunia.md) · [run](../production/runs/2026-10-27T0334+0800-cycle-4.md) · target [v2](../design/concepts/80-otus-sunia-v2.png).

The exact age, wing length, crouching pose, sex ratio, universal nest choice and cross-subspecies geometry remain unknown; the low supported pose and near-equal adult display are conservative design decisions. `scientifically_verified=false`.

Source type: primary comparative taxonomy/morphology. Species/stage: adult museum specimens and genus-level morphology; historical *Ciccaba nigrolineata* = current *Strix nigrolineata*. Valuable facts: wood-owl round head/no ear tufts; *C. nigrolineata* mean wing 289 mm (n=3); left/right external-ear data 13/18 mm (two specimens) and table means 11/12 mm with no dermal flap; text associates *C. nigrolineata* with *C. huhula* through exceptional black-and-white barred plumage. Location: article pp.471–478, especially pp.472–476 and Tables I–II. Design use: supports rounded tuftless head and historical-name mapping; does not justify juvenile age or a sex height formula.

- Source facts vs design inference: evidence supports dark tuftless adults with horizontal barring, a bare epiphyte platform example, and an age-unspecified cream/dusky mesoptile. Alert supported crouch, short soft wing geometry and near-equal adult display are conservative design choices. Exact juvenile age/flight ability, sexed linear dimensions, toe geometry and universal nest choice remain unknown. New 1/reused 2; scientifically_verified=false.

- **EGO-NSW2018** — NSW Environment, Energy and Science. “Eastern Grass Owl — profile.” Last updated 21 June 2018. https://threatenedspecies.bionet.nsw.gov.au/profile?id=10819 . Accessed 2026-10-27. Source type: Australian government species account, page actually read at lines 198–215. Species/stage: Australian adult/habitat/nesting context. Valuable facts: medium 35-cm ground-dwelling owl; dark-brown/buff/yellow-orange upperparts with fine silvery spots; white male underparts, buffy larger female underparts with sparse dark spots; very long mostly bare legs; tall upright roost posture; tall grass/swamp/sedge habitat; ground nests in trodden grass with vegetated tunnels. Limits: no juvenile chronology or sexed linear measurement table. Confidence: high for broad Australian identification/ecology; not a primary growth study. Design use: adult contrast, bare long legs, upright posture and ground-grass context.
- **EGO-LIN2007** — Lin, Wen-Loung; Wang, Yin; Tseng, Hui-Yun. “Initial Investigation on the Diet of Eastern Grass Owl (*Tyto longimembris*) in Southern Taiwan.” *Taiwania* 52(1):100–105, 2007. DOI https://doi.org/10.6165/tai.2007.52(1).100 ; author full text actually read at https://www.researchgate.net/publication/251774474_Initial_Investigation_on_the_Diet_of_Eastern_Grass_Owl_Tyto_longimembris_in_Southern_Taiwan . Accessed 2026-10-27. Source type: primary field study. Species/stage: Taiwanese population/subspecies context, post-breeding nests and mobile young. Valuable facts: three nests found in grass-cutting; young were fully mobile, some flight-stage; disturbance ended observation. Limits/conflict: not nominate Australia, no exact age or plumage sequence, and not applicable to the chosen 55-day captive-reference stage. Confidence: high for those three nests, low for cross-subspecies transfer. Design use: explicit exclusion boundary only; no Taiwanese mobility/flight inference transferred.
- Reused: **EGO-DUNLOP2016** (Australian captive full text pp.169–172/Table 3/Fig.1), **EGO-MIK2013-102** (printed pp.102–103/PDF pp.104–105), **EGO-AVIBASE2026** (current/historical name mapping).
- Source facts vs design inference: adults and ground-grass context follow Australian sources; the golden 55-day-inspired mesoptile follows one captive family and remains a concept, not a wild field reconstruction. Exact wing readiness, body geometry, pose and universal sex ratio remain unknown. New 2/reused 3; scientifically_verified=false.
- Links: [research record](103-tyto-longimembris-v2.md) · [cycle run](../production/runs/2026-10-27T0533+0800-cycle-2.md) · target [v2](../design/concepts/103-tyto-longimembris-v2.png).


### ABO-COLLAR-BOESMAN-2019
N. J. Collar & Peter Boesman. 2019. “The taxonomy of certain Asio and Bubo owl species in Africa and Arabia.” Bulletin of the African Bird Club 26(2):155–169. https://www.researchgate.net/publication/335292194_The_taxonomy_of_certain_Asio_and_Bubo_owl_species_in_Africa_and_Arabia . Accessed 2026-10-27. Source type: primary taxonomic synthesis using museum specimens, plumage, morphometrics and vocal evidence. Actual author-uploaded full text read, especially pp155–160, Tables 1–2 and Figs1–3. Applies to adult A. abyssinicus (nominate plus graueri), not juvenile development. Key facts: authors measured 20 A. abyssinicus; pooled means bill 33.8 mm, wing 322.8 mm, tail 178.1 mm versus A. o. otus 28.5/286.2/155.1 mm and A. o. wilsonianus 26.6/281.7/156.7 mm; the paper supports species status and states A. abyssinicus is roughly 15% larger with distinct plumage and shorter hoot. Design decision: do not treat A. abyssinicus as a palette-swapped A. otus; retain its heavier proportions and species-specific adult markings. Limitations: specimen means are not a male/female standing-height formula; paper supplies no nestling stage, sex dichromatism or live posture. Confidence high for adult relative size/taxonomy, none for juvenile anatomy. Related: [species #112](species/112-asio-abyssinicus.md), [cycle 3](../production/runs/2026-10-27T0533+0800-cycle-3.md).


### PSO-MACAULAY-2010
Martin Kennewell / Macaulay Library, ML201585161 (IBC 1180864), 18 July 2010, Mt Makiling Forest Reserve, Los Baños, Laguna, Philippines, 773 m. https://macaulaylibrary.org/asset/201585161 . Accessed 2026-10-27. Source type: institutional media archive holding a contributor field video/metadata. Actual asset page read, especially lines63–115: locality/date, media notes “juvenile in canopy screeching (begging for food?) at 0400hrs,” while archive Age and Sex fields are both “Not specified.” Applies to a free-canopy later juvenile action context only. Design support: a supported canopy-perching/vocal juvenile is plausible; no nest is implied. Limits: contributor’s begging interpretation is explicitly uncertain, the page supplies no hatch age, sex, plumage measurements or visible-frame description in the text reader, and the displayed player reported 0:00; therefore this source is not used for color, iris, wing or tail geometry. Confidence moderate for field context/date/locality, low for developmental inference. Related: [species #122](species/122-otus-megalotis.md), [cycle 4](../production/runs/2026-10-27T0533+0800-cycle-4.md).


## 2026-10-27 — #124 *Ketupa philippensis* v2 evidence increment

- **PEO-URRIZA-2017** — Rolly C. Urriza, Juan Carlos T. Gonzalez, Michael S. Sanchez & Juancho B. Balatibat, “Post breeding biology of the Philippine Eagle-owl *Bubo philippensis* (Strigiformes: Strigidae) on Luzon,” *The Pelican Express* 8(15), February 2017, pp. 2–6. URL: https://www.researchgate.net/publication/319207927_Post_breeding_biology_of_the_Philippine_Eagle-owl_Bubo_philippensis_Strigiformes_Strigidae_on_Luzon (PDF viewed 2026-10-27; cover/page 2 and abstract text actually read; later PDF page screenshots intermittently returned 403). Source type: primary field study. Taxon/stage: Luzon *Bubo*/*Ketupa philippensis*, nestling through departure. Facts: weekly observations over five weeks at an Angono, Rizal nest plus Balara visit; authors state the young may take at least 70 days to fledge and that a pair may reuse the same nest/site. Design support: choose a late nestling/near-fledged stage with formed wing/tail and branch-supported pre-flight action; do not infer an exact plumage sequence from the abstract. Limits: single observed young, weekly intervals, PDF access incomplete; no sexed morphometrics or complete day-by-day feather measurements. Confidence: high for the authors’ stated ≥70-day fledging conclusion; moderate for generalization.
- Reused **PEO-OCHAVO-2012**, **PEO-ARTUSO-2015**, **PEO-AVIBASE-MAPPING**. Their actual field text/images support adults with yellow eyes/gray bill/rufous-brown shaft pattern, a later young with formed wing/tail and secure perch grip, and the historical *Bubo* → current *Ketupa* name mapping. They do not justify a fixed sex-size ratio or universal nest form.
- Design decision: approximately 70-day late nestling/near-fledged juvenile, not a natal chick; near-equal adult display with only subtle female fullness; Luzon/northern unit only. Scientific verification remains false.
- Related run: [2026-10-27T0533+0800 cycle 5](../production/runs/2026-10-27T0533+0800-cycle-5.md).


## 2026-10-27 — #127 *Megascops albogularis* v2 evidence increment

- **WTSO-MACAULAY-1980** — Ted Parker/Cornell Lab Macaulay Library, ML21782, recorded 24 July 1980 at Cerro Chinguela, Cajamarca, Peru. URL: https://macaulaylibrary.org/asset/21782 (actual archive page read 2026-10-27). Source type: institutional primary field audio record. Taxon/stage: adult, sex unknown; media notes say 1–2 adults, territorial paired, advertising in humid cloudforest/edge. Design support: humid montane cloud-forest edge setting and paired-adult context only. Limits: audio record, no usable plumage geometry or sex-size evidence; “male/female” in notes does not provide measurements. Confidence: high for locality/habitat/behavior, none for visual morphology.
- Reused **WTSO-PERUAVES-PHOTOS2019** (actual adult/juvenile photos previously viewed), **WTSO-MIK2013-249** (uploaded guide printed pp. 249–250 / PDF pp. 251–252, text actually read), and **WTSO-NCBI714938-MAPPING**. Adult limits: no projecting ear tufts/fluffy head, white throat ovals, tarsi to toe bases, female qualitatively heavier but no height formula. Juvenile: Piura warm-buff fluffy stage with diffuse bars and formed lower wing; exact age and iris color unresolved.
- Fresh search also found current EcoRegistros adult records and the existing PeruAves page; no additional juvenile-development sequence was available, so they were not promoted as new developmental evidence.
- Related run: [2026-10-27T0533+0800 cycle 6](../production/runs/2026-10-27T0533+0800-cycle-6.md).

## 2026-10-27 — #132 *Strix davidi* v2 evidence increment

- **DAV-CLEMENTS-2022** — Cornell Lab of Ornithology, Clements Checklist “Updates & Corrections — October 2022”, published 2022, accessed 2026-10-27. URL: https://www.birds.cornell.edu/clementschecklist/introduction/updateindex/october-2022/updates-corrections-october-2022/ . Source type: authoritative checklist update. Taxon/stage: `Strix davidi` / historical `Strix uralensis davidi`; all ages. Valuable fact: cites Scherzinger 2004 and Scherzinger et al. 2014 in the Pere David's/Ural Owl treatment; supports name mapping and documents current-list conflict. Section: species update references around page line 1085–1087. Limits: no morphology, sex-size or juvenile-development evidence. Confidence: high for checklist treatment, none for visual details.
- Reused **DAV-SCHERZINGER-2014** (Scherzinger, Fang, Sun & Klaus, *Ornithologischer Anzeiger* 53:54–93; primary Lian Hua Shan field work 1995–2013; web full text actually reopened 2026-10-27) and **DAV-MIK2013-348** (uploaded Mikkola guide, printed pp.348–349/PDF350–351, earlier text actually read). Primary paper facts used: radial adult face pattern rather than bold concentric rings; blackish-brown eyes/yellow bill/long tail/feathered toes; approximately four-week young in Fig.11 with gray woolly barred mesoptile, brown mask, dark iris/pinkish lids and pale bill. Exact age and sex-size ratio remain uncertain.
- **DAV-SCHERZINGER-2004-BHL** — Scherzinger, “Remarks on Sichuan Wood Owl…”, *Bulletin BOC* 125:275–286, BHL page https://www.biodiversitylibrary.org/page/40056584 . Search lead found via Clements but current fetch returned 403; **unverified lead**, not used as validated evidence.
- Design decision: preserve v1 layout and box-supported young; remove dotted/concentric adult facial halos, reduce excessive white wing spotting, darken central tails with subtle irregular scribbles; science remains false.
- Related run: [2026-10-27T0728+0800-cycle-1](../production/runs/2026-10-27T0728+0800-cycle-1.md).

### 2026-10-27 #135 *Otus silvicola* v2 source reuse audit

- Reused `WSC-MEES-2006` after fresh targeted reread of Naturalis pp.113–115 / species account and Fig.8 context: examined material includes fledglings whose remiges and rectrices remain basally sheathed; the type's immature transition is fully feathered with fine loose barring and few adult shafts. Exact age is not given. This supports formed but softer/less adult-regular wing and tail, not erasing them.
- Reused `SLSO-SANGSTER-2013` after fresh PLOS full-text read of Diagnosis: Morphology, lines 322–323: *O. silvicola* colder gray-brown upperparts with broader shaft streaks/bars; pale grayish-cinnamon/buffy-gray breast only slightly darker than belly sides with white belly center; tarsal feathering covers toe bases. It supplies no juvenile development or population sex ratio.
- Stable source IDs added: 0; corrected: 0; reused: 2. Design application only: reduce v1 immature's crisp adult-like white wing patch rows while retaining the evidence-supported fully feathered transitional stage. Scientific verification remains false.

## #136 Megascops petersoni revision v2 — accessed 27 Oct 2026
### CIN-FREILE-CASTRO-2013
Juan F. Freile & Diego F. Castro (2013), “New records of rare screech owls (Megascops) and pygmy owls (Glaucidium), with taxonomic notes and a conservation assessment of two globally imperilled species in Ecuador,” Cotinga 35:5–10. Original URL: https://neotropicalbirdingandconservation.org/wp-content/uploads/2025/07/35-cotinga-online-02-new-records-of-rare-screech-owls-megascops-and-pygmy-owls-glaucidium-with-taxonomic-notes-and-a-conservation-assessment.pdf . Accessed 2026-10-27. Source type: primary field/museum compilation. Scope: *Megascops petersoni*, adult/locality/habitat; account p.6, Table 1 p.8 (PDF pp.1 and 3). Three new field localities and two museum records; records span primary forest, secondary forest and a stunted ridge at 1,234–2,100 m. It supports a humid cloud-forest/secondary-growth perch, not nest architecture or juvenile morphology. High habitat/locality confidence; ecological segregation explicitly unresolved. Supports #136 v2 run, not scientific verification.

### CIN-MACAULAY-458758931
Cornell Lab of Ornithology, Macaulay Library asset ML458758931, Cinnamon Screech-Owl (*Megascops petersoni*), photographed 3 May 2022. Original URL: https://macaulaylibrary.org/asset/458758931 . Accessed 2026-10-27. Source type: institutional specimen-equivalent field photograph; adult/unknown sex. Actual pixels viewed: dark eye, gray-green bill, overwhelmingly cinnamon underparts, restrained facial pale areas, barred cinnamon wing/tail, natural mossy-branch support. Single posture, lighting and no supplied age/sex limit exact color, tuft and sex inference. Supports v2 color/pattern restraint and perch anatomy; does not support immature stage, sex ratio or nest. Copyright photo linked, not copied.

### Reuse mapping
Reused `CIN-FITZPATRICK-ONEILL-1986`, original Wilson Bulletin pp.3–6 (PDF pages 2,4–6) freshly reread. Critical correction is application, not source correction: virtually no white; slight-if-any sexual dimorphism; nearly adult first-year specimen with paler barred down. New2/reused1/corrected0. Mikkola upload unavailable in current file index after two searches; no page claim. [Run](../production/runs/2026-10-27T0728+0800-cycle-4.md), [card](../knowledge/species/136-megascops-petersoni.md), target v2.

## #137 Otus alfredi revision v2 — accessed 27 Oct 2026
### FSO-HUTCHINSON-2007
Robert Hutchinson, James Eaton, Bram Demeulemeester & Frank E. Rheindt (2007), “Observations of Flores Scops Owl Otus alfredi on Flores, Indonesia, with a first description of its vocalisations,” Forktail 23:184–187. Original URL: https://static1.squarespace.com/static/5c1a9e03f407b482a158da87/t/5c20f94f4d7a9c6cf87abdf2/1545664849106/Hutchinson-FloresScops.pdf . Accessed 2026-10-27. Primary field observation; adults and habitat. Species account pp.184–186 directly supports compact form, short rounded tufts, solid rufous face/no black rim, distinct white eyebrows, rufous breast/flanks, finely vermiculated pale lower belly, no black streaks, white scapular markings, small spaced primary notches, yellow irides/legs/feet, and remnant tall trees adjoining primary montane forest. It does not describe juvenile development, nest or sex ratio. High adult identity/habitat confidence; visual geometry limited by field views.

### FSO-MACAULAY-62333921
Cornell Lab of Ornithology, Macaulay Library asset ML62333921, Flores Scops-Owl (*Otus alfredi*), Ross Gallardy, undated page metadata. Original URL: https://macaulaylibrary.org/photo/62333921 . Accessed 2026-10-27. Institutional field photograph; adult/unknown sex. Actual pixels viewed: compact silhouette, yellow eyes, rufous unbordered face and gray-pale lower underparts on a natural branch. Flash/dark exposure, hidden feet and no supplied age/sex limit exact palette, white-spot and soft-part inference. Supports broad identity/perch check only.

### FSO-WIDODO-COX-RASMUSSEN-1999 v2 interpretation correction
Original Forktail pp.17–21, Tables 1–2 freshly reread. Prior terse “solid rufous upperparts” design summary was incomplete: Table 2 explicitly documents white scapular feathers with 1–2 clear dark-brown outer-web blotches and brownish tips, plus small spaced whitish primary triangles. v2 therefore bounds/patterns oversized blank v1 patches rather than deleting legitimate white. Juvenile MZB 30.086 retains natal down; exact age/iris/flight remain unknown. New2/reused1/corrected1 interpretation. [Run](../production/runs/2026-10-27T0728+0800-cycle-5.md), [card](../knowledge/species/137-otus-alfredi.md), target v2.

| BUO-JOHNSON-AGING-2019 | Johnson et al., “Aging Key for Juvenile Burrowing Owls (*Athene cunicularia*)”, 2019, Burrowing Owl Conservation Society of British Columbia, https://burrowingowlalliance.ca/wp-content/uploads/2025/08/Johnson-et-al.-2019.-Aging-Key-for-Juvenile-Burrowing-Owls-Athene-cunicularia.pdf | Photographic aging key; captive-reared/released western *A. c. hypugaea* plus wild Alberta checks | Full 34-page key read: daily photos support age estimates ±2–3 days; mean hatching-to-fledging about 42 days (reported range 41–47); dated series includes day 29 before fledging. | Regional western subspecies and managed birds; not universal. Supports a 29-day prefledging, ground-supported juvenile, not exact flight ability or all populations. |
| BUO-CORNELL-LIFE-2026 | Cornell Lab of Ornithology, All About Birds “Burrowing Owl—Life History”, accessed 2026-10-27, https://www.allaboutbirds.org/guide/Burrowing_Owl/lifehistory | Institutional synthesis; hatch/nest/behavior | Read page states grayish-white down, helpless/eyes closed at hatch and a 44–53-day nestling period; burrow nesting and ground behavior supported. | Nestling duration differs from the 41–47-day aging-key range; 29 days is safely prefledging under both. No dated plumage sequence on page. |
| BUO-COSEWIC-2017 | COSEWIC, *Assessment and Status Report on the Burrowing Owl Athene cunicularia in Canada*, 2017, Government of Canada, https://www.canada.ca/en/environment-climate-change/services/species-risk-public-registry/cosewic-assessments-status-reports/burrowing-owl-athene-cunicularia-2017.html | Government status report; Canadian *hypugaea* ecology | Reproduction section read: about 44-day nestling period, then juveniles use satellite burrows; female broods and male provisions; first three weeks are food-sensitive. | Canadian regional scope; ecology and broad stage, not color plate or precise molt chronology. |
| BUO-CORNELL-OVERVIEW-2026 | Cornell Lab of Ornithology, All About Birds “Burrowing Owl—Overview”, accessed 2026-10-27, https://www.allaboutbirds.org/guide/Burrowing_Owl/overview | Institutional synthesis; adult identity, habitat, sex statement | Read page: small sandy owl, yellow eyes, long-legged ground activity, open grassland/desert, burrow/dirt-mound use; explicitly says sexes are the same size. | Sex-size statement conflicts with Mikkola. Plate therefore keeps adults near-equal rather than converting either claim into a fixed ratio. |

Female qualitatively a little larger. Downy chick white; mesoptile resembles a very pale Tawny Owl with yellowish eyes and weaker talons. Semi-desert, rocky ravines with water and palm groves. | Predates the 2015 split. Facts are used only for the widespread population later named *S. hadorami*, not current *S. butleri*. Synthesis, no dated growth sequence or exact mesoptile flight ability. |
| DEOW-KIRWAN-2015 | Guy M. Kirwan, Manuel Schweizer & José Luis Copete, “Multiple lines of evidence confirm that Hume’s Owl *Strix butleri*…is two species…”, *Zootaxa* 3904(1):28–50, 2015, DOI https://doi.org/10.11646/zootaxa.3904.1.2 ; open copy https://nnhc.huji.ac.il/sites/default/files/nnhc/files/15-zootaxa-kirwan_schweizer_copete_compressed.pdf | Primary taxonomic paper; adult/juvenile specimens, morphology, genetics, distribution | Full relevant pp.37–45 read. Diagnoses *S. hadorami* by light rufous cross-barring below, uniform pale facial discs and near-equal dark/pale tail bars. Holotype female 320 mm; specimen masses males 140–183.5 g, females 214–220 g with small samples; authors report no pronounced sexual dimorphism, female average only slightly larger. Widespread old “butleri” populations from Egypt/Israel/Jordan through Arabia are *hadorami*. | Museum samples small; do not impose a fixed sex ratio or living eye color from dried skins. The listed juvenile specimen has no developmental description; Mikkola supplies the bounded mesoptile fact. High for taxonomy/diagnosis, not full growth chronology. |
| DEOW-IOC-2015-SPLIT | IOC World Bird List, English Name Updates v5.4, 24 Oct 2015, https://www.worldbirdnames.org/new/updates/english-names/ | Authoritative checklist change; current nomenclature | Actual update line read: current *Strix butleri* is Omani Owl, following the *butleri*/*hadorami* sorting by Kirwan et al. and Robb et al. Current project concept therefore uses *Strix hadorami* for the widespread Desert Owl. | Taxonomy only; no morphology or juvenile facts. |
| DEOW-EBIRD-2026 | Cornell Lab/eBird, “Desert Owl *Strix hadorami*”, accessed 2026-10-27, https://ebird.org/species/humowl1 | Institutional current-name field account and media index | Account identifies a shy medium desert-canyon owl, sandy brown overall, pale lightly marked underparts, barred wing/tail and yellow eyes; nests in cliff crevices/caves. | Synthesis and media gateway, not a measured developmental series. Used for living-color and supported crevice context only; exact nest geometry and juvenile age remain unspecified. |

Dull reddish-brown above, dense white spotting on crown/neck/mantle and wings; barred tail; variable white-spotted brown breast bands, white lowerparts and pale-barred flanks; dark face, narrow white eyebrows, large white throat half-collar, dark-brown eyes, greenish-yellow bill, feathered tarsi. Mesoptile is dark brown above/below with mixed dark-brown/white eyebrows; wing and tail feathers are explicitly stated to be as adult. Primary forest near streams, also open woodland, cultivation and settlements, mostly lowlands to 1100 m. | Guide synthesis, not a dated growth series. The adult-like juvenile wing/tail exception is species-specific; exact mesoptile age, flight ability, nest site and juvenile iris are not supplied. Female-size direction is qualitative and rests on one sexed measurement, so the plate uses near-equal adults with only subtle female fullness. |
| SPBO-FITZSIMONS-2012 | James A. Fitzsimons, Erik Meijaard, Iwan Hunowu, Dewi Prawiradilaga, Janelle L. Thomas & Johny S. Tasirin, “Diet of the Speckled Boobook *Ninox punctulata* in north Sulawesi, Indonesia”, *Forktail* 28:169–171, 2012, https://sawfish-kazoo-6w4a.squarespace.com/s/Speckled-Boobook.pdf | Primary field/pellet study; sheltered roost and foraging context | Entire three-page note read. Pellets/prey remains came from a known sheltered roost in an abandoned dwelling at 220 m beside rainforest, river and agricultural land. Beetles and introduced rats were identified; authors infer flexible use of forest edge, scrub and agricultural habitats, while explicitly cautioning that the small sample does not represent the complete diet. The discussion quotes the species as short-tailed, heavy-tarsed and Athene-like with toe bristles. | One roost and a small, degraded pellet sample; supports habitat mosaic, heavy tarsi and a sheltered roost context, not nest design, breeding stage or universal diet proportions. The concept uses natural stream-side forest-edge support rather than converting the building into an invented nest. |
| SPBO-EBIRD-2026 | Cornell Lab/eBird, “Speckled Boobook *Ninox punctulata*”, accessed 2026-10-27, https://ebird.org/species/spehao1 | Institutional current-name field account and media index | Account actually read via current indexed description: distinctive medium owl with rounded head, prominent white eyebrows, dark eyes, white belly and widely spotted gray upperparts/crown. | Concise synthesis/media gateway, not a primary developmental study. Used only to cross-check living adult identity and current English/scientific-name pairing. |
