## 2026-10-27 #174 Cinereous Owl — *Strix sartorii* v1

### CIO-AOS-2021-P263
- Title: Sixty-second Supplement to the American Ornithological Society’s Check-list of North American Birds
- Authors/institution: Chesser et al.; American Ornithological Society
- Year/date: 2021
- URL/DOI: https://doi.org/10.1093/ornithology/ukab037
- Accessed: 2026-10-27
- Type: peer-reviewed checklist supplement
- Scope: *Strix sartorii* taxonomy, habitat and range; adults/general
- Location read: checklist p.263 species account (web lines 808–818)
- Facts: AOS treats *S. sartorii* separately from *S. varia*; original combination is *Syrnium nebulosum* var. *Sartorii*. Habitat is pine-oak/pine forest at 1400–2500 m. Split support includes vocal, mtDNA, plumage and habitat differences.
- Limits/conflicts/confidence: authoritative taxonomy/range; not an age-development or sex-dimorphism source. Historical range is broader than recent detections. High confidence for taxonomy and habitat.
- Design/check decision: current species identity and Mexican montane pine-oak background; do not use it to invent juvenile detail or female size.
- Links: [card](species/174-strix-sartorii.md) · [run](../production/runs/2026-10-27T1836+0800-cycle-3.md)

### CIO-BARROWCLOUGH-2011
- Title: Phylogeography of the Barred Owl (*Strix varia*): Species Limits, Multiple Refugia, and Range Expansion
- Authors: Barrowclough et al.
- Year/date: 2011; *The Auk* 128(4):696–706
- URL/DOI: https://doi.org/10.1525/auk.2011.11057
- Accessed: 2026-10-27
- Type: primary phylogeographic research
- Scope: Mexican *sartorii*, North American *varia*, Central American *fulvescens*; adults/specimens
- Location read: abstract and methods/results excerpt
- Facts: analyses used nuclear and mitochondrial loci; Mexican *sartorii* was separated from northern *varia* by *fulvescens* in most trees. Authors recognized three species and reported about 4–5% genetic divergence among them; Mexican sample consisted of nine historical Jalisco skins.
- Limits/conflicts/confidence: old skin sample and no recent Mexican specimens; genetic/taxonomic support only, not live color, sex, nest or juvenile development. High confidence within stated sampling limits.
- Design/check decision: supports separate project identity but supplies no rendered trait.
- Links: [card](species/174-strix-sartorii.md) · [run](../production/runs/2026-10-27T1836+0800-cycle-3.md)

### CIO-EARBIRDING-2015
- Title: Mexico’s Mystery Owl
- Authors: Nathan Pieplow and Andrew Spencer
- Date: 2015-06-07
- URL: https://earbirding.com/blog/archives/4905
- Accessed: 2026-10-27
- Type: dated field observation, photographs and recordings
- Scope: adult and age-unknown juvenile *S. sartorii* at Rancho La Noria, Nayarit, 2015-06-05
- Location read: page sections “The find”, “Visuals”, comparison figure/captions (lines 5–10, 47–54)
- Facts: page documents and labels a juvenile photograph. Adult has a notably pale, gray facial disc without obvious darker concentric rings, cold gray coloration, dark sepia streaks on off-white underparts, cold gray collar, and toe feathering extending to claw bases.
- Limits/conflicts/confidence: extremely small field sample; juvenile exact age and flight status are not stated on the page. Strong direct visual evidence for the photographed stage/adult individual, not universal variation.
- Design/check decision: use a fully supported age-unknown older juvenile; no exact age/flight claim. Preserve pale gray face, cold palette, sepia streaks and feathered toes.
- Links: [card](species/174-strix-sartorii.md) · [run](../production/runs/2026-10-27T1836+0800-cycle-3.md)

### CIO-BAIRD-RIDGWAY-1874-P29
- Title: A History of North American Birds, Vol. III (original *sartorii* account)
- Authors: Baird, Brewer and Ridgway
- Year: 1874
- URL: https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/54169/pg54169-images.html
- Accessed: 2026-10-27
- Type: historical original descriptive work
- Scope: adult female type-like specimen from Mirador near Veracruz; historical name *Syrnium nebulosum* var. *sartorii*
- Location read: printed p.29, footnote 21 (web lines 424–433 and 15106)
- Facts: blackish-sepia and clear white plumage, face without darker concentric rings; adult female specimen had much darker/less reddish brown than *nebulosum*, nearly black underpart markings, with the same underlying barred/streaked pattern.
- Limits/conflicts/confidence: one historical specimen and old comparative terminology; useful diagnostic boundary, not population-wide sex or age evidence.
- Design/check decision: cold blackish-sepia/white direction and no facial rings; do not infer female-specific coloration.
- Links: [card](species/174-strix-sartorii.md) · [run](../production/runs/2026-10-27T1836+0800-cycle-3.md)

### CIO-EBIRD-2026
- Title: Cinereous Owl *Strix sartorii* species account
- Institution: Cornell Lab / eBird
- Accessed: 2026-10-27
- URL: https://ebird.org/species/barowl13
- Type: current institutional field-identification account
- Scope: adults/general; Mexico
- Location read: species identification and habitat text
- Facts: large dark-eyed owl of western/central Mexican pine and pine-oak forests; cold brown/gray tones, barred breast and vertically streaked belly; rare/local and known from few sites.
- Limits/conflicts/confidence: concise field account, not a developmental study and no quantitative sex difference. High for adult field diagnosis/habitat.
- Design/check decision: retain dark eyes, cold gray-brown palette, breast bars/belly streaks and montane forest; no invented sex contrast.
- Links: [card](species/174-strix-sartorii.md) · [run](../production/runs/2026-10-27T1836+0800-cycle-3.md)


## 2026-10-27 — #107 *Strix fulvescens* v4 recovery revision

- Reused verified source IDs: `FVO-EISERMANN-FIELD2026`, `S107-EBIRD-FULOWL1`, `S107-CAYAYA-2026`, `S107-OWLPAGES-2020`.
- Fresh read: `S107-BIRDLIFE-2026` — BirdLife International, Fulvous Owl species factsheet, https://datazone.birdlife.org/species/factsheet/fulvous-owl-strix-fulvescens, accessed 2026-10-27. Institutional conservation/taxonomy account; treats *S. fulvescens* as a species and notes vocal, morphological and coloration differences from historical *S. varia* lumping. Limit: dynamic page yielded no juvenile developmental sequence or sex ratio; used only for current identity/status boundary.
- Fresh web checks of eBird, CAYAYA field photos/account and Owl Pages confirmed adult warm buff/rufous direction, dark/brown eyes, rounded no-tuft head, cloud/highland forest, and an older fluffy photographed juvenile. The existing 2026-10-16 direct-photo review remains the actual juvenile visual evidence.
- Correction: recursive tree inspection found v3 at `design/concepts/107-strix-fulvescens-v3.png`; current cycle is therefore v4, not v3. No previous version is overwritten or recounted.

## 2026-10-27 #120 Rufescent Screech-Owl · *Megascops ingens* v3 evidence update

### RIS-ORDONEZ-2022-LEAD — corrected full-read status
- Title: *Primer registro del polluelo y nuevos datos del nido del Autillo Rojizo (Megascops ingens) (Strigiformes: Strigidae) en Zamora Chinchipe, sureste de Ecuador*
- Authors: Leonardo Ordóñez-Delgado, Freddy Ramón Castillo, Angel Pineda Díaz
- Year / publication: 2022; *Neotropical Biodiversity* 8:301–304
- DOI / original URL: https://doi.org/10.1080/23766808.2022.2123729 ; https://ikiam.researchcommons.org/neotropical-diversity/vol8/iss1/35/
- Accessed: 2026-10-27
- Type: primary field report; open-access CC BY-NC 4.0
- Scope/stage: southeastern Ecuador nest and a single chick estimated by authors at 20–30 days; adult shown in Figure 1c.
- Location actually read/viewed: publisher-repository 11-page PDF, printed pp.301–304; Figure 1a–d on p.302; separate 1200×1488 Figure 1 image.
- Verified facts: chick light-beige base, dark-brown face/eye contour, whitish forehead, faint brown crown/nape/mantle bands stronger on mantle/scapulars, residual white natal down; considerably developed primary/secondary/tertiary feathers barred light/dark brown like adults; gray bill with pale-yellow egg-tooth vestige; open dark-brown eyes/black pupils; no characteristic adult tufts. Simple bare cavity in a dead stump at lower-montane evergreen forest edge beside pasture, with a slight bed of dry/damp vegetation.
- Correction: the earlier index entry accurately recorded the initial 403 limitation. This run obtained the public publisher-repository PDF and figure without bypassing access controls, so full text/figure evidence is now verified.
- Limits/conflicts/confidence: age is an author estimate, not known hatching date; one nest/chick cannot establish universal clutch, dimensions or population-wide plumage. Abstract/body stump-height wording differs; no exact height is rendered. High for this observation, low for universal generalization.
- Decision: use this exact documented stage and retain formed barred remiges; do not apply a blanket short-wing rule. No exact-day or flight claim.
- Links: [research record](species/120-megascops-ingens-v3.md) · [run](../production/runs/2026-10-27T2126+0800-cycle-3.md)

### RIS-MIKKOLA-2013-P229
- Title: *Owls of the World: A Photographic Guide*
- Author: Heimo Mikkola
- Year: 2013
- Source: user-uploaded guide
- Accessed: 2026-10-27
- Type: photographic field guide / secondary synthesis
- Scope/stage: *Megascops ingens* adult, downy chick and mesoptile
- Location actually read/viewed: printed p.229 / PDF p.231, including Glenn Bartley adult photograph.
- Verified facts: small tufts; females average 28 g heavier; dark olive-tawny/reddish/gray-brown upperparts with fine pale spots and narrow dark vermiculation; honey-brown eyes; olive-yellow bill/cere; densely feathered tarsi to toe bases; whitish-buff downy chick and dusky-mottled mesoptile.
- Limits/conflicts/confidence: synthesis does not supply a dated growth series; old two-subspecies account predates current checklist treatments. Qualitative sex-mass direction does not justify a fixed height ratio. Moderate-high for identification boundary, not a science pass.
- Decision: adults retain small tufts, deep honey-brown eyes and feathered tarsi; female only subtly fuller. Juvenile stage comes from the 2022 primary observation.
- Links: [research record](species/120-megascops-ingens-v3.md) · [run](../production/runs/2026-10-27T2126+0800-cycle-3.md)


## 2026-10-27 — #118 *Megascops cooperi* v4 science revision

### PSC-MIKKOLA-2013-P210
- Heimo Mikkola, 2013, *Owls of the World: A Photographic Guide*, 2nd ed., printed p.210 / PDF p.212; user-uploaded copy accessed/read 2026-10-27 and adult photograph actually viewed. Source type: photographic guide. Species/stage: adult *Megascops cooperi*; juvenile explicitly undescribed. Facts: 23–26 cm, 145–170 g; female average about 20 g heavier; rather prominent tufts; pale gray-brown, fine shaft streaks/coarse bars; true white scapular shoulder band plus second pale closed-wing covert band; yellow eye, greenish bill/cere, feathered tarsi. Limitation: female mass direction is not a height formula; no juvenile details; exact individual variation unknown. Design support: retain both bands, natural prominent tufts, subtle female fullness. [Species card](species/118-megascops-cooperi-v4.md) · [run](../production/runs/2026-10-27T2126+0800-cycle-4.md). Confidence high for printed description/photo.

### PSC-AVESCR-2025
- Aves de Costa Rica, *Megascops cooperi*, https://avesdecostarica.com/Megascops-cooperi.html ; accessed/read 2026-10-27. Source type: secondary field-reference page. Species/stage: adults and juvenile. Read lines 28–36: gray-brown adult with fine dusky vermiculation/black shaft streaks; juvenile gray-buff above, whitish below grading to deeper buff breast, finely dusky-barred above/below; forest-edge/deciduous woodland/savanna/mangrove habitat. Page’s AI illustration explicitly excluded as evidence. No exact age/action/sex ratio. Design support: juvenile palette and fine-bar rather than decorative tile texture. [Species card](species/118-megascops-cooperi-v4.md). Confidence medium.

### Existing-source application
- `PSC-KOMAR-STUFKENS-2012` institutional PDF printed/PDF pp.65–66 and Figure 4 re-downloaded, text read and page viewed: older fledgling ground defensive wing-spread after falling when startled, difficulty flying, exact age unknown. Reused, not duplicated.
- `PSC-OWLPAGES-2020` current lines 50–77 opened/read: two real adult pale wing bands, fine irregular shaft/vermiculation pattern, yellow eye, greenish bill and feathered tarsi. Reused, not duplicated.

## 2026-10-28 — #172 *Tyto castanops* v2 science revision

### TMO-DEBUS-WOOD-2007-P49-52
- Title: “Growth of a Captive Nestling Masked Owl *Tyto novaehollandiae*”
- Authors/institution: S.J.S. Debus and C. Wood; Australian Field Ornithology 24:49–53
- Year / URL / accessed: 2007; https://afo.birdlife.org.au/afo/index.php/afo/article/viewFile/610/596 ; accessed 2026-10-28
- Type: primary captive growth observation with photographs
- Taxon/stage: nominate *T. n. novaehollandiae* growth series; explicitly compares cream down reported for Tasmanian *T. n. castanops*
- Actually read: printed pp.49–52 / PDF pp.1–4; Plate 11 captions and text pp.50–51; Plate 12 caption p.51.
- Source facts: the authors interpret the photographed chick at about 4 weeks as downy with a developing mask, and at about 5 weeks as having thick second/mesoptile down and a well-developed mask with feathers not yet visible through the down. A probably ~11-week new fledgling was fully grown with only traces of down on feather tips. The paper says dark-morph Tasmanian chicks apparently have cream rather than buff mesoptile down, citing Higgins 1999.
- Limits/conflicts/confidence: one captive nominate-subspecies female, estimated ages because hatch date was not recorded, unlimited-food conditions, and the Tasmanian cream-down statement is a secondary comparison. High for the photographed individual/stage sequence; moderate for transfer of cream hue to *castanops*; it does not justify a universal age or exact flight threshold.
- Design/check decision: v1’s nearly spherical downy bird is not retained as an “advanced nestling.” The revision uses an explicitly approximate five-week, thick cream-down nestling with a developed mask and no visible flight feathers; science remains false.
- Links: [species card](species/172-tyto-castanops.md#v2-science-revision--2026-10-28) · [run](../production/runs/2026-10-28T0029+0800-cycle-2.md)

Reused source IDs: `TMO-MIKKOLA-2013-P110`, `TMO-ABSA-2019-P2`, and `TMO-AUSMUSEUM-2024`. No duplicate entries were created. The Australian Museum page was freshly read for current adult sex direction, young white/cream color, hollow chamber material and forest/woodland habitat. Scientific verification remains false.

## 2026-10-28 — #164 Desert Owl · *Strix hadorami* v2 science revision

### DEOW-BENDOV-2017-P285-307
- Title: “Breeding biology of Desert Owl in Israel”
- Authors: Amir Ben Dov, Aviam Atar, Erez Baruchi, Kochav Levi & Nir Sapir
- Publication/year: *Dutch Birding* 39:285–307, 2017
- Original URL: https://www.researchgate.net/publication/320517643_Breeding_Biology_of_Desert_Owl_in_Israel
- Accessed/read: 2026-10-28; full text and plate captions actually read.
- Source type / taxon / stage: primary field breeding study of current *Strix hadorami*; nestlings and fledglings from two Israeli sites.
- Verified facts: printed p.286 plate 392 shows adult male, adult female and fledgling; p.288 plate 394 records first flight at about 30 days; p.289 plates 395–398 document an adult feeding an approximately 30-day nestling, an approximately 35-day fledgling and a 70–75-day fledgling with breast feathers appearing; the chronology records eyes partly open at day 7, fully open at day 9, wing-flapping at 20–25 days, standing on the nest shelf at 25 days, first flight around 30 days, leaving the nest site around 34 days, and body feathers growing at 70–75 days. Nest sites were cliff ledges/crevices in desert gorges.
- Limits/conflicts/confidence: the authors note limited prior information and use Tawny Owl comparisons for some ageing; observed dates vary, and the paper text/plate captions read do not establish juvenile iris colour. High for the reported observations and plate captions, not a universal timetable or iris rule.
- Design/check decision: retain a fully supported pale mesoptile on a rocky ledge and avoid a false flight-age claim; correct v1’s unsupported bright adult-yellow juvenile iris to a deliberately neutral, shaded uncertain presentation. This remains a concept, not a scientific pass.
- Links: [species card](species/164-strix-hadorami.md#v2-science-revision--2026-10-28) · [run](../production/runs/2026-10-28T0029+0800-cycle-4.md)

The old-name mapping remains explicit: Mikkola’s widespread-population account under historical *S. butleri* applies to current *S. hadorami*, not the current Omani Owl. Scientific verification remains false.

## 2026-10-28 — #177 São Tomé Scops Owl · *Otus hartlaubi* v1 evidence increment

### STSO-MIKKOLA-2013-P136

- **Title / author / year:** *Owls of the World: A Photographic Guide*, 2nd ed. / Heimo Mikkola / 2013.
- **Type:** uploaded photographic field guide; species account and plate.
- **Original locator:** user-uploaded PDF; printed p.136 / PDF p.138, accessed 2026-10-28.
- **Applies to:** *Otus hartlaubi*; adults of both morphs and mesoptile juvenile.
- **Actually read/viewed:** page text extracted and page pixels rendered/viewed.
- **Source facts:** 17–19 cm; very small tufts; females a little larger/heavier. Grey-brown morph is very dark with blackish shaft streaks, large whitish scapular areas, largely unbarred primaries, nearly unbanded tail, whitish underparts with fine brown/rufous barring and converging blackish shaft streaks; deep yellow eyes, yellow bill/cere; distal quarter of tarsus unfeathered. Rufous morph is dark reddish-brown with prominent black markings. Downy chick is not described; mesoptile of both morphs is paler than adult and fully finely barred. Habitat includes humid primary cloud forest, secondary forest and clove/mango plantation edges.
- **Limitations/conflicts:** no exact juvenile age, iris chronology, wing/tail development, flight threshold, nest geometry or sex-specific plumage. High confidence for printed description; unknown fields remain unknown.
- **Design decision:** same-morph adults with only a modest documented female size/fullness difference; fully supported paler finely barred mesoptile with neutral-dark shaded iris and no flight/nest claim.

### STSO-BIRDLIFE-RDB-1985-P326

- **Title / institution / year:** “Sao Tome Scops Owl, Otus hartlaubi” / ICBP–IUCN Red Data Book account / 1985-era compilation.
- **Original URL:** https://birdlifedata.blob.core.windows.net/red-data-books/Sao_Tome_Scops_Owl_afr_rdb.pdf (accessed 2026-10-28).
- **Type:** conservation synthesis citing historical specimens and field records.
- **Applies to:** distribution, habitat, diet and breeding evidence; juvenile/fledgling stage.
- **Actually read:** PDF pp.326–327 (web PDF pages 1–2), especially ecology lines 30–37.
- **Source facts:** highest recorded density in secondary forest at Fortunato; food records include insects, grasshoppers, beetles, moth and a few bones. Fledglings in October indicate breeding from late August/early October; a broader September–February season is rainfall-based inference.
- **Limitations/conflicts:** old conservation status and nomenclatural year are not used as current taxonomy; fledglings lack plumage, exact age, nest and soft-part description.
- **Design decision:** supports forest/secondary-growth background and confirms young occur, but juvenile anatomy remains anchored to Mikkola’s mesoptile.

### STSO-GABONBIOTA-INAT-2021

- **Title / creator / date:** São Tomé Scops-Owl image detail / hiporame / photographed 2021-12-17.
- **Original URL:** https://gabonbiota.org/portal/imagelib/imgdetails.php?mediaid=483515 ; medium image https://inaturalist-open-data.s3.amazonaws.com/photos/174205079/medium.jpg (accessed 2026-10-28).
- **Type:** georeferenced living-adult photograph mirrored from iNaturalist open data.
- **Applies to:** living adult, São Tomé, Mé-Zóchi.
- **Actually read/viewed:** metadata page and medium image pixels.
- **Source facts:** compact very dark reddish-brown adult with subtle low tufts, deep yellow eyes, small pale scapular marks and fine irregular pale barring/streaking in natural foliage.
- **Limitations/conflicts:** single adult; sex and morph assignment not independently verified; no juvenile or size comparison. Visual cross-check only.
- **Design decision:** constrain adult palette, low tuft silhouette and irregular rather than mechanical markings; do not infer sex from this image.

### Source-to-design map

- **Adult morphology / sex direction:** STSO-MIKKOLA-2013-P136; living-photo cross-check STSO-GABONBIOTA-INAT-2021.
- **Juvenile stage:** STSO-MIKKOLA-2013-P136 only; STSO-BIRDLIFE-RDB-1985-P326 confirms fledglings but lacks morphology.
- **Habitat/background:** STSO-MIKKOLA-2013-P136 and STSO-BIRDLIFE-RDB-1985-P326.
- **Unknown by evidence:** exact juvenile age, iris chronology, remex/tail sequence, flight threshold, nest geometry and sex-specific plumage.


## 2026-10-28 — #178 Seychelles Scops Owl · *Otus insularis* v1 evidence increment

### SSCO-MIKKOLA-2013-P138

- **Title / author / year:** *Owls of the World: A Photographic Guide*, 2nd ed. / Heimo Mikkola / 2013.
- **Locator:** user-uploaded PDF, printed p.138 / PDF p.140, accessed 2026-10-28.
- **Type:** photographic field guide species account; page text and pixels actually read/viewed.
- **Facts:** 20 cm, 130–159 g; tiny often invisible tufts; female more than 20 g heavier. Dull yellowish-brown crown with white spots/black streaks, mottled buff-brown upperparts, dark-edged white scapular band, pale underparts with broad blackish streaks, yellow/green-yellow eyes, dark-tipped grey-yellow bill, totally bare tarsi and long pale toes. Juvenile said to resemble adult but not well known. Habitat: hilly forest 250–600 m and wooded upland gardens.
- **Limitations:** no juvenile age, soft parts, continuous feather sequence or precise activity threshold; exact nest is not described on this page.
- **Decision:** older adult-like but softer juvenile, neutral-dark shaded iris, fully supported; do not invent downy chick.

### SSCO-CURRIE-2004-BREEDING

- **Title / authors / year:** “The breeding biology of the Critically Endangered Seychelles Scops-owl *Otus insularis*: consequences for conservation and management” / Dave Currie et al. / 2004.
- **DOI / URL:** https://doi.org/10.1017/S0959270904000140 ; full text read at https://www.researchgate.net/publication/248692719_The_breeding_biology_of_the_Critically_Endangered_Seychelles_Scops-owl_Otus_insularis_consequences_for_conservation_and_management (accessed 2026-10-28).
- **Type:** primary field study, Bird Conservation International 14:123–137.
- **Facts read:** observations on 12 pairs, eight nests; all nests in tree cavities 7–25 m high with one egg or chick; incubation 3–4 weeks; fledging period 4–6 weeks from two nests; 11 solitary fledglings from eight territories; fledglings remained on territory at least three months. Upland Mahé mist forest study area 140–710 m.
- **Limitations:** no detailed fledgling plumage or iris chronology; 4–6 week fledging interval derives from two nests and is not a license to invent exact feather state.
- **Decision:** a simple high-cavity context and an older supported juvenile are evidence-supported; no nest lining or exact-age label.

### SSCO-CURRIE-2002-SIZE

- **Title / authors / year:** “Sexual size dimorphism in the critically endangered Seychelles Scops Owl *Otus insularis*” / Currie, Mateman, Lessells & Fanchette / 2002.
- **Locator:** Ringing & Migration 21; abstract read in the full-text research page above, lines 1294–1307, accessed 2026-10-28.
- **Type:** primary biometric study; 31 live birds, 30 molecularly sexed (6 females, 24 males).
- **Facts:** females generally heavier and larger; plumage colour varied but was unrelated to sex, with no evidence of distinct colour morphs.
- **Limitations:** abstract-level reading; unbalanced sex sample; no fixed visual percentage for body height or detailed plumage dimorphism.
- **Decision:** use same-plumage adults with clear but moderate female fullness, not colour-coded sexes and not a universal rule.

### Source-to-design map

- Adult morphology: SSCO-MIKKOLA-2013-P138.
- Female size direction and no sex-linked colour: SSCO-CURRIE-2002-SIZE.
- Juvenile/nest stage: SSCO-MIKKOLA-2013-P138 plus SSCO-CURRIE-2004-BREEDING.
- Unknown: exact juvenile age, iris/soft parts, continuous feather state, flight threshold, nest lining.

## 2026-10-28 — #11 *Aegolius funereus* v3 evidence increment

### BOR-KOUBA-2015-FLEDGING
- **Title / authors / year:** “Factors Affecting the Duration of Nestling Period and Fledging Order in Tengmalm’s Owl (*Aegolius funereus*): Effect of Wing Length and Hatching Sequence” — Kouba, Bartoš, Korpimäki & Zárybnická, 2015.
- **URL / DOI:** https://journals.plos.org/plosone/article/file?id=10.1371%2Fjournal.pone.0121641&type=printable · https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pone.0121641
- **Accessed:** 2026-10-28. **Type:** primary camera/chip/radio-telemetry study; printable paper and Results actually read.
- **Species / stage:** *A. funereus*, late nestlings through fledging; 98 young at 27 nests.
- **Facts:** nest period 27–38 d (mean 32.4±2.2); duration negatively related to wing length; wing growth continues after fledging, so fledglings leave with incomplete feather growth.
- **Decision:** supports a 30–33 d cavity-edge/recent fledgling with formed but incompletely grown wings, full support and no confident flight.
- **Limits / confidence:** no fine plumage map or universal exact day; high confidence for age/action boundary.

### BOR-KORPIMAKI-HAKKARAINEN-2012-P7
- **Title / authors / year:** *The Boreal Owl: Ecology, Behaviour and Conservation of a Forest-Dwelling Predator* — Erkki Korpimäki & Harri Hakkarainen, 2012.
- **URL:** https://api.pageplace.de/preview/DT0400.9781139574600_A23868104/preview-9781139574600_A23868104.pdf
- **Accessed:** 2026-10-28. **Type:** Cambridge University Press research synthesis; preview pp.6–7 / PDF pp.21–22 actually read.
- **Species / stage:** *A. funereus*, adults and late nestling/fledgling.
- **Facts:** fledglings are dark brown overall, face darker/sootier, very distinct white eyebrows, some white spots on scapulars and wing coverts; Finnish adult samples show females substantially heavier on average than males while wing-length difference is modest.
- **Decision:** juvenile must not inherit adult white underpart streaking; female may read fuller/heavier without a fixed height multiplier.
- **Limits / confidence:** synthesis/photographic caption, not a day-by-day sequence; high confidence for diagnostic direction.

### Reused sources
- **BOR-MIK2013-440** — Mikkola 2013 printed pp.440–443 / uploaded PDF pp.442–445 re-read: adults untufted, yellow-eyed, yellowish-horn bill, spotted brown upperparts and streaked whitish underparts; downy chick white/thin, later mesoptile uniformly dark chocolate-brown with white eyebrows/chin-side streaks; eyes pale yellow-grey then yellower before fledging. Six-subspecies account retained as historical scope, not a fixed artwork subspecies.
- **BOR-KOUBA-2014** — PLOS ONE DOI 10.1371/journal.pone.0097504 re-read: entrance perching 24–34 d (mean 28); climbing begins around day 20, reaching entrance 26–30 d; wing growth incomplete at fledging.

**Mapping:** `Aegolius funereus` = Tengmalm’s Owl (Old World name) = Boreal Owl (North American English).  
**Source counts:** new 2 / reused 2 / corrected 0.  
**Unknowns retained:** exact depicted day, continuous feather map, quantified standing-height ratio, subspecies, natural-cavity versus box geometry. `scientifically_verified=false`.  
**Related:** [research card](species/11-aegolius-funereus-v3.md) · [run](../production/runs/20261028T0813+0800-cycle-3.md).

## 2026-10-28 · #23 *Psiloscops flammeolus* v4 evidence increment

- **FLOW-POWERS-1996-JRR** — Leon R. Powers, Allen Dale, Peter A. Gaede, Chris Rodes, Lance Nelson, John J. Dean & Jared D. May (1996), “Nesting and Food Habits of the Flammulated Owl (*Otus flammeolus*) in Southcentral Idaho,” *Journal of Raptor Research* 30(1):15–20. URL: https://paperzz.com/doc/8481120/journal-of-raptor-research-30-15%E2%80%9320 ; accessed 2026-10-28. Type: peer-reviewed primary field study. Applies to *Psiloscops flammeolus* (old combination *Otus flammeolus*), nests and nestling period. Read pages 15–20, especially abstract p.15 and Results pp.16–17: 24 nesting attempts used 22 cavities; 83% were in dead trees, mean entrance 6.8 cm; phenology calculations used 22 days for the nestling period. Limitation: no age-specific plumage description or evidence of day-11 flight. Design decision: reused woodpecker-like cavity and no flight inference; does not supply plumage details. Confidence: high for nest context, none for day-specific plumage.
- Reused `FLOW-FOSSILBEDS-2007`: species-specific field/seminar account records three 11-day owlets at 30–40 g with white natal down and beginnings of gray. Limitation: observational public account, not a peer-reviewed growth series; used only for coarse stage/color boundary.
- Reused `FLOW-CORNELL-ID-2026`, `FLOW-CORNELL-LIFE-2026`, `FLOW-ORI-2026` for adult identity, similar-looking sexes, cavity nesting and white-down hatch condition. No fixed sex height ratio inferred.
- Linked research card: [#23 v4](species/23-psiloscops-flammeolus-v4.md); image version `design/concepts/23-psiloscops-flammeolus-v4.png`; run `production/runs/20261028T0951+0800-newbatch-cycle-4.md`.


## 2026-10-28 · #24 *Glaucidium californicum* v3

- `NPO-FRYE-JAGEMAN-2012-WJO` — Frye, Graham G.; Jageman, Harry R. 2012. “Post-Fledging Ecology of Northern Pygmy-Owls in the Rocky Mountains.” *Wilson Journal of Ornithology* 124(2):199–207. Record and abstract: https://agris.fao.org/search/en/providers/122535/records/65df806d4c5aef494fe30f2a . Accessed 2026-10-28. Source type: peer-reviewed primary field study. Applies to Montana/Idaho Northern Pygmy-Owl population, post-fledging juveniles and parental attendance; paper uses broad *Glaucidium gnoma*. Abstract actually read: 16 successful nests; data from five radio-marked and three additional family groups; earliest young hunting at 9 days post-fledging; female attendance 9–30 days and male 31–34 days post-fledging. Limitation: abstract does not give day-9 plumage measurements and does not resolve current species limits. Design decision: an explicitly dependent day-9 fledgling on a fully supported branch, not airborne or independent. Confidence high for behavior/timing, limited for plumage.
- Reused `NPO-CORNELL-ID-2026`, `NPO-CORNELL-OVERVIEW-2026`, `NPO-PEREGRINE-2026` for adult identity, nape eyespots, sex-mass versus height boundary, fledging around one month and initially awkward short hops. No mass difference converted into a standing-height ratio.
- Research card: [#24 v3](species/24-glaucidium-californicum-v3.md); run `production/runs/20261028T1008+0800-newbatch-cycle-5.md`; image `design/concepts/24-glaucidium-californicum-v3.png`.


## 2026-10-28 · #26 *Glaucidium siju* v3 evidence increment

- `CUPO-PYLE-ETAL-2004-JFO` — Pyle, Peter; McAndrews, Amy; Vélez, Pilar; Wilkerson, Robert L.; Siegel, Rodney B.; DeSante, David F. 2004. “Molt patterns and age and sex determination of selected southeastern Cuban landbirds.” *Journal of Field Ornithology* 75(2):136–145. PDF: https://www.birdpop.org/docs/pubs/Pyle_et_al_2004_Molt_Patterns_and_Age_and_Sex_Determ_of_Selected_SE_Cuban_Birds.pdf . Accessed 2026-10-28. Primary banding/specimen study. Species account printed p.138 / PDF page 3 actually read. Applies to southeastern Cuban *G. siju*, juvenal plumage and adult sex criteria. Evidence: 48 individuals, 127 captures, 41 specimens; juveniles downy with unmarked backs, streaked underparts, indistinct crown spots; females wing 95–101 mm and reddish-brown, males 84–94 mm and grayish-brown with some reddish-tinged males. The paper marks eye-color aging as tentative and does not give exact juvenile day, flight ability, tail growth or subspecies assignment. Design decision: an age-unknown juvenal-plumaged supported bird; muted iris only as uncertain direction; no exact-day or flight claim. Confidence high for body-plumage and sex direction, limited for eye and development chronology.
- Reused `CUPO-GARRIDO-2002` (printed pp.47–49 / PDF pages 3–5) for 152-specimen geographic/measurement context and natural/woodpecker/palm cavity ecology; `CUPO-EBIRD-ID-2025`, `CUPO-BIRDSCARIBBEAN-2020`, `CUPO-BOW-2020` for current identity/range. Correction: the v2 evidence gap “no primary juvenile series found” is superseded by the newly located Pyle account, but exact age remains unknown.
- Linked research card: [#26 v3](species/26-glaucidium-siju-v3.md); image version `design/concepts/26-glaucidium-siju-v3.png`; run `production/runs/20261028T1020+0800-newbatch-cycle-6.md`.


## 2026-10-28 · #27 *Glaucidium passerinum* v3 evidence increment

- `EUPO-EGIR-2026` — “Воробьиный сыч / *Glaucidium passerinum*,” *Птицы России и сопредельных стран* (Birds of Russia and adjacent countries), https://www.egir.ru/bird/165.html . Accessed 2026-10-28. Detailed scientific secondary species synthesis; Description and Molt sections actually read. It cites Schönn 1980 for nestling development: feather pins prominent day 8 and opening day 12; head/back appear feathered day 18; day 20 is evenly feathered except numerous pins on tail and facial disc; day 27 fully feathered. Mesoptile is dense, chocolate-brown and nearly unspotted on head/neck/back; underparts whitish with sparse brown longitudinal spots. Iris is brown initially and pale yellow by fledging. Limits: the underlying 1980 primary pages were not accessible in this run; day-20 iris and individual variation are not specified. Design decision: a supported 20-day cavity nestling with no mature tail/flight claim and muted iris treated as inference, not fact. Confidence medium-high for sequence, limited for exact color.
- Reused `EUPO-PACENOVSKY-SOTNAR-2010` for cavity/fledging ecology, `EUPO-SOVADINOVA-2024` to avoid juvenile sex assignment, `EUPO-LUNDBERG-1986` for moderate adult reversed size dimorphism, and `EUPO-ADW-2018` for broad development context. Correction: v2's exact-age gap is narrowed to a distinct 20-day nestling design, but primary-page and iris uncertainty keep science false.
- Linked research card: [#27 v3](species/27-glaucidium-passerinum-v3.md); image version `design/concepts/27-glaucidium-passerinum-v3.png`; run `production/runs/20261028T1035+0800-newbatch-cycle-1.md`.

V3 therefore depicts a fully supported cream-white/pale-rufous downy clamberer with short soft rounded emerging wing tips and concealed tail beside a cavity/hollow-log or palm-sheath context. Exact continuous feather sequence, population/subspecies transfer and quantified sex difference remain unknown; `scientifically_verified=false`.

## #54 *Bubo africanus* · batch 2026-10-29T0153+0800 cycle 3 v3 research increment

| Stable ID | Title / author or institution / year / URL | Source type and scope | Actually read evidence | Limits / design decision |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| SEO-OWLPAGES-2020 | Deane Lewis, [Spotted Eagle Owl ~ *Bubo africanus*](https://www.owlpages.com/owls/species.php?s=1280), updated 2020-10-26; accessed 2026-10-29 | Specialist owl synthesis citing HBW, König & Weick and Mikkola; adults, breeding and juvenile timing | Description and Breeding lines 63–77 read. Typical birds have yellow eyes, conspicuous tufts, fine dark barring; young leave/explore at about 4–6 weeks and are normally flying by 7 weeks. It says females are heavier. | Secondary synthesis without a day-by-day feather plate. The female-heavier statement conflicts in visual implication with BDI/SANBI “alike” wording and does not override SEO-OSTRICH-RSD-2008, where only tarsus differed significantly in the sampled adults. Used only to cross-check the 4–6/7-week locomotor boundary; no fixed sex ratio. Confidence: medium. |

Reused and freshly read on 2026-10-29: SEO-SANBI-2026 lines 62–69; SEO-BDI-2022 lines 40–66; SEO-OSTRICH-RSD-2008 abstract/results. SANBI supplies the decisive species-specific correction to v2: at about six weeks the chicks leave the nest but are not yet able to fly; wing feathers are well grown, tail feathers are about half final length, and some down remains on head and underparts; flight begins at about seven weeks. BDI independently supports 4–6-week exploration and visible fluffy down with weaker tufts. The primary morphometric paper found only tarsus length significantly different between sexes in its small regional sample, so adults remain near-equal rather than universally “female larger.” Bubo bubo development literature found during search is not transferred to this species. Uploaded Mikkola guide was not readable in this run, so no new page claim is made.

Design correction: v2’s supported downy juvenile was conservative but its hidden/very short tail and “short rounded wing” boundary undershot the newly reread SANBI six-week account. V3 targets a supported approximately-six-week preflight explorer with well-grown folded wings, a visible half-length tail and remaining head/underpart down. Exact feather geometry, individual age, regional variation and quantitative adult sex ratio remain unknown. New sources 1; reused stable sources 3; corrected design inference 1; science remains false. [Species card](../knowledge/species/54-bubo-africanus.md) · [run](../production/runs/20261029T0228+0800-cycle-3.md).

Adult male is subtly deeper rufous; female slightly fuller at comparable height, not dramatically taller.

Mass is not converted into a fixed standing-height ratio.

V3 adds only short, rounded, down-softened juvenile feather clusters while retaining the documented low-mobility buff-down cavity posture. Exact age, continuous feather chronology and universal Palmchat-nest use remain unknown. Science false.

## 2026-10-29 · #66 *Tyto capensis* v3 — recent-fledgling dorsal pattern and wetland tunnel revision

### AGO-BLSA-RDB-2025
- Stable ID: AGO-BLSA-RDB-2025
- Title/institution/date: African Grass Owl / BirdLife South Africa Red Data Book / current 2025 assessment page.
- URL: https://www.birdlife.org.za/red-data-book/red-list/african-grass-owl/
- Accessed: 2026-10-29.
- Type and scope: regional conservation institution species account; current name, adult and fledgling identification, habitat and nesting structures.
- Evidence actually read: Biology & Ecology, Taxonomy, Identification and Ecology; web lines 122–145. The page treats *T. capensis* as current and monotypic under Kemp, while noting some authors combine it with *T. longimembris*. Adults have a white dense facial disc, dark teardrop eyes, whitish bill, dark olive-brown upperparts with tiny white tip-spots, pale underparts, buff-feathered legs and grey feet. At fledging juveniles are darker and lack white dorsal spots; a buff/rufous facial disc is explicitly unreliable. Tall rank damp grass/sedges, tunnels, caves and landing platforms support the ground setting.
- Limits/conflicts/confidence: regional South African assessment; fledgling statement cites Kemp rather than a measured growth series, and rufous-face exceptions include breeding adults. High for regional identification/habitat, medium-high for fledgling dorsal-pattern direction; not an exact age chronology.
- Design decision: v3 uses a grounded fully feathered recent fledgling with dark unspotted dorsum, no forced rufous face, and a concealed wetland grass tunnel/landing platform. Science remains false.

### Reused/corrected evidence
- AGO-ADW-2014 (University of Michigan ADW, Physical Description and Parental Investment, lines 65–67 and 113–124, reread 2026-10-29): white first down and dense pale-brown/buff second coat are earlier stages; mobility begins around five weeks and learning flight around seven. v3 selects the later recent-fledgling stage, so it does not reuse the v1/v2 downy body.
- SEO-OSTRICH-RSD-2008 (Ansara-Ross et al., DOI 10.2989/OSTRICH.2008.79.1.11.366, pp.2–3, Table 1/2 and Results; ResearchGate OCR lines 279–369, reread 2026-10-29): corrected scope to include *T. capensis*, not only the earlier #54 application. In the Gauteng road-carcass sample, females were heavier and longer-bodied; sexes were not distinguishable by dichromatic difference. This supports only a modest female body-volume direction, not a fixed standing-height ratio or range-wide rule.
- AGO-WHITTINGTON-2012 and AGO-PEREGRINE-2026 remain reused for rank wetland grass, ground tunnels and approximate movement/flight timing; no precise feather chronology is inferred.

### Name mapping and unresolved items
- Current name: *Tyto capensis* (Smith, 1834). No old genus combination is required by the repository record. BirdLife South Africa notes a taxonomic-treatment conflict: some authors have combined it with *T. longimembris*; this plate follows the repository/IOC-separated *T. capensis* concept.
- Still unknown: universal sex ratio/visual scaling, exact individual fledgling age, exact wing/tail emergence sequence and geographic variation. These remain explicit unknowns and prevent scientific pass.

### Full imagegen prompt

```text
Use case: scientific-educational. Asset type: owl-pet species concept plate. Create a polished 3:2 science-revision plate in the repository’s established realistic-cute natural-history game concept style for African Grass Owl. Show exactly three owls with exact readable text: title “AFRICAN GRASS OWL”, italic scientific name “Tyto capensis”, bottom labels “ADULT MALE”, “RECENT FLEDGLING”, “ADULT FEMALE”. Adults: no ear tufts; dense white heart-shaped facial disc with a subtle yellow-buff/dark speckled border, dark teardrop eyes, whitish to pale gray bill; dark olive-brown upperparts with tiny white spots at feather tips; white to pale-buff underparts with scattered dark spots; long buff-feathered legs and greyish feet naturally supported. Sexes are visually alike; based on a Gauteng carcass sample, show the adult female only modestly fuller/longer-bodied than the male, never an exaggerated standing-height ratio. Juvenile: a recent fledgling around the learning-to-fly period, but grounded with wings folded and no flight pose; fully feathered and structurally capable of moving through grass, darker than the adults, unspotted dark upperparts lacking the adults’ tiny white dorsal spots, deep golden-buff lower plumage, dark eyes and pale bill. Do not force a rufous face because BirdLife South Africa says that is not a reliable age trait. Do not depict white first down, baby wing buds, mature adult dorsal spotting, or unsupported exact day-by-day feather chronology. Place the group within tall rank wetland grass and sedges on damp substrate. Show a concealed ground nest/runway system with trampled grass tunnel, small cave-like shelter and stable grass landing platform; no tree hollow, rock ledge, eggs or exposed open nest. All feet must have natural support and correct perspective. No extra birds, chicks, text, logos, borders or watermarks.
```

Links: [species dossier](../knowledge/species/66-tyto-capensis.md), [v1](../design/concepts/66-tyto-capensis-v1.png), [v2](../design/concepts/66-tyto-capensis-v2.png), [cycle2 run](../production/runs/20261029T0642+0800-cycle-2.md).


## 2026-10-29 · #68 *Tyto tenebricosa* v3 — sex-volume and three-month fledgling reconciliation

### GSO-BILNEY-2012
- Stable ID: GSO-BILNEY-2012
- Title: Reversed sexual dimorphism and altered prey base: the effect on sooty owl (*Tyto tenebricosa tenebricosa*) diet.
- Authors/source/year: Bilney, White & Cooke / Australian Journal of Zoology 59(5):302–311 / 2012; DOI https://doi.org/10.1071/ZO11101 .
- Accessed: 2026-10-29; primary peer-reviewed ecology study.
- Evidence actually read: publisher article lines 159–190, especially Introduction lines 188–189. Nominate southeast-Australian females cited at 1000–1200 g and males 550–700 g; the paper frames this as exceptionally strong reversed sexual dimorphism. Hollow dependence and tall wet-forest context are also explicit.
- Limits: masses are cited from Higgins/Hollands within a diet paper, not this paper’s own morphometric experiment; values support clear body-volume direction but not a standing-height ratio or every subspecies. High for nominate sex direction, medium for exact visual scaling.
- Design decision: v3 makes the female clearly fuller/longer-bodied on comparable support; no numeric height ratio.

### GSO-NSWSC-2008
- Stable ID: GSO-NSWSC-2008
- Title/institution/date: Sooty Owl *Tyto tenebricosa*: Review of Current Information in NSW / NSW Scientific Committee / September 2008.
- URL: https://www.environment.nsw.gov.au/sites/default/files/2024-09/sc-sooty-owl-tyto-tenebricos-review-report.pdf
- Accessed: 2026-10-29; government review PDF, 6 pages.
- Evidence actually read: PDF p.1 species description and taxonomy; p.2 key habitat/breeding (viewer P0–P1, lines 13–50). Dark sooty-grey, black eyes, grey face, fine white spotting, pale finely barred belly, fully feathered legs to large toes; nominate NSW subspecies. Rainforest/moist eucalypt gullies, old large smooth-gum hollows (over 30 cm wide, potentially very deep); nestling period about three months and post-fledging dependence up to five months.
- Limits: government synthesis, regional NSW scope; it does not give a continuous juvenile feather sequence. High for morphology/habitat/nest duration, medium for fine fledgling feather detail.
- Design decision: v3 uses an old living smooth-gum hollow in a moist tree-fern gully and a fully feathered recent fledgling at the hollow lip, not a neonatal chick.

### Reused evidence and conflicts
- GSO-ABSA-2019 (single-page ABSA/HANZAB-based banding profile, reread from existing index): fledged juveniles nearly complete adult-like but darker with little down; supports residual crown/underpart tufts only.
- GSO-OWLPAGES-2026 (description/breeding lines 67–79 freshly reread): black eyes, cream bill, grey-brown dark-rimmed face, sooty white-spotted plumage, feathered legs, large hollow, ~3-month fledging. Its “sooty-grey down” refers to early chicks and is not used to repaint a recent fledgling as all-down.
- Mikkola pp.114–115 remains indexed: natal-down colour conflict is irrelevant to the later stage selected here. Current name remains *Tyto tenebricosa*; plate follows nominate *T. t. tenebricosa*, not the smaller *multipunctata* taxon under alternative species treatments.
- Unknown: exact individual age, residual-down placement, universal female:male visual ratio and New Guinea/subspecies variation. Science false.

### Full imagegen prompt

```text
Use case: scientific-educational. Asset type: owl-pet species concept plate. Create a polished 3:2 science-revision plate in the established realistic-cute natural-history game concept style for Greater Sooty Owl. Show exactly three owls with exact readable text: title “GREATER SOOTY OWL”, italic “Tyto tenebricosa”, bottom labels “ADULT MALE”, “RECENT FLEDGLING”, “ADULT FEMALE”. Use the nominate southeast-Australian concept, not the smaller Lesser Sooty Owl. Adults: no ear tufts; rounded heart-shaped grey-brown facial disc with a heavy dark charcoal rim, black eyes, whitish-cream bill; dark sooty blackish-brown upperparts and breast with fine white spots, spots somewhat larger over back/wing coverts; paler grey-brown belly with fine grey barring/mottling; legs densely feathered to large bare grey-brown toes and dark claws. Sexes share plumage but strong evidence supports a substantially heavier/larger female: show her clearly fuller and longer-bodied than the male while keeping both feet on comparable support; do not convert mass ranges into a precise standing-height ratio. Juvenile: recent fledgling after the approximately three-month nestling period, fully feathered with developed folded wings and tail, darker than adults, adult-like juvenile plumage with only a few small residual down tufts on crown and lower belly/thighs; black eyes, pale bill, no natal chick body, no baby wing buds, no flight pose. Place all three naturally supported at the entrance/branch lip of a very large deep hollow in an old living smooth-barked gum within a moist rainforest/eucalypt gully with tree ferns and dense mid-storey. The juvenile may crouch low at the hollow lip; adults stand on adjacent stable wood. No cave-only implication, no eggs. Correct feet/perspective. No extra birds, chicks, text, logos, borders or watermarks.
```

Links: [species dossier](../knowledge/species/68-tyto-tenebricosa.md), [v1](../design/concepts/68-tyto-tenebricosa-v1.png), [v2](../design/concepts/68-tyto-tenebricosa-v2.png), [cycle3 run](../production/runs/20261029T0648+0800-cycle-3.md).


## 2026-10-29 · #69 *Tyto novaehollandiae* v3 — 11-week fledgling and strong sex-direction application

- Active external reread: AMO-DEBUS-WOOD-2007 original developmental study (Australian Field Ornithology 24:49–53; ResearchGate full text pp.49–52), AMO-AUSTRALIAN-MUSEUM-2024, TMO-ABSA-2019-P2, and the current NSW Environment profile (id10820).
- Correction/application expansion: the earlier AMO-DEBUS-WOOD-2007 index entry emphasized 4–5-week mesoptile. The actually read printed pp.51–52 also reports a captive nominate dark-morph female recent fledgling, estimated ~11 weeks: fully grown, traces of down on head/facial ruff/underparts, darker and more heavily marked than dark-morph parents. Hatching date was unknown and release occurred later, so ~11 weeks remains an estimate, not a precise clock.
- Sex evidence: ABSA/HANZAB ranges and Australian Museum/NSW accounts agree females are significantly larger and generally darker; apply clear body-volume/darkness direction on comparable support, not a numeric height ratio.
- Nest/habitat: Australian Museum and Museum Victoria support a deep bare tree chamber lined by soil/sand/soft wood mulch; use one mainland eucalypt hollow, not a universal nest.
- Name/morph boundary: current repository species *Tyto novaehollandiae*, nominate mainland dark morph as one bounded example; Tasmanian *castanops* remains excluded from this plate. Exact individual age, residual-down placement, morph prevalence and universal ratio remain unknown; science false.

### Full imagegen prompt

```text
Use case: scientific-educational. Asset type: owl-pet species concept plate. Create a polished 3:2 science-revision plate in the established realistic-cute natural-history game concept style for mainland Australian Masked Owl. Exactly three owls and exact readable text: title “AUSTRALIAN MASKED OWL”, italic “Tyto novaehollandiae”, bottom labels “ADULT MALE”, “RECENT FLEDGLING”, “ADULT FEMALE”. Use nominate mainland dark morph as one bounded example, not Tasmania and not a universal morph. Adults: no ear tufts; dark eyes; pale horn bill; broad heart-shaped chestnut-buff facial disc with a dark brown rim and darker shading around eyes/bill; dark brown to chestnut upperparts with fine pale/white speckling; rufous-to-pale underparts with dark spotting; fully feathered legs and powerful feet. Females are significantly larger and generally darker: show the female clearly fuller/longer-bodied and somewhat darker than the male, with comparable support; do not turn measured mass/wing ranges into an exact standing-height ratio. Juvenile: a recent fledgling around the study’s estimated 11-week stage, fully grown and fully feathered with developed folded wings/tail, darker and more heavily marked than the dark-morph parents, with only small traces of down clinging to crown/facial ruff/underparts; no all-down chick, baby wing buds, flight pose or exact age label. Place all three at a large deep natural tree hollow in mainland eucalypt forest, with the juvenile crouched on a stable hollow-floor/lip of soil, sand and soft wood mulch, adults on adjacent stable wood. Feet and perspective anatomically supported. No eggs, extra birds, chicks, text, logos, borders or watermarks.
```

Waterhouse, “Development of independence in Powerful Owl *Ninox strenua* fledglings in suburban Sydney,” *Australian Field Ornithology* 32:143–153 (2015), https://www.researchgate.net/publication/281851615_Development_of_independence_in_Powerful_Owl_Ninox_strenua_fledglings_in_suburban_Sydney ; accessed 2026-10-29. Printed pp.145–147/Fig.1 actually read: two young left the hollow on 5 and 14 August; one flew out and crashed into a shrub, both were observed flying by 17 August; adult plumage began developing by December. Primary two-fledgling urban study; strong for action capability, not universal feather geometry.
- **POWERFUL-ABSA-2019 (new, HANZAB synthesis):** Australian Bird Study Association, *Bird in the Hand: Powerful Owl* (March 2019), https://absa.asn.au/wp-content/uploads/2019/09/Powerful-Owl.pdf ; p.1 actually read. Fledges in juvenile plumage; adult-like immature transition begins 3–4 months after fledging; female marginally smaller, with overlapping morphometrics. Narrow dorsal bars, ventral chevrons, yellow-orange eyes, feathered legs/large yellow feet; initially whiter/downier juvenile head and underparts; large old-tree hollow, two-month nestling period and prolonged dependence. Government synthesis; no continuous feather series.
- **Reused:** POWERFUL-MV-2026 (Museums Victoria, lines 67–81 reopened), VIC-RAPTORS-2023 printed p.85/PDF p.10, POWERFUL-AM-2023, POWERFUL-OWLPAGES-2020, and WHSO-MIKKOLA-2013 printed pp.450–451/PDF pp.452–453.
- **Corrected design inference:** v2's very large male/female gap is reduced to a modest male body-volume direction; the recent fledgling keeps developed folded wings/tail because actual young flew soon after leaving the hollow, but retains a downier white head/underparts, dark eye patches and broader paler juvenile wing bars. Exact age, linear ratio and feather chronology remain unknown; science false.

The Bogotá defensive raised-wing observation is used only for pose, not as an exact age transfer. Adults retain only a subtle unquantified female-size direction. Exact individual feather geometry, sex ratio, iris/bill chronology and cross-population transfer remain unknown; science false.

- **MAR-HANANE-CHERKAOUI-2014 (new, primary; transfer limited):** Saâd Hanane & Sidi Imad Cherkaoui, “Breeding of the Marsh Owl (*Asio capensis tingitanus*) in a strictly forest area of Morocco: a chance or a choice?”, *Go-South Bulletin* 11:46–49 (2014), https://www.researchgate.net/publication/262057939_Breeding_of_the_Marsh_Owl_Asio_capensis_tingitanus_in_a_strictly_forest_area_of_Morocco_a_chance_or_a_choice ; printed pp.46–49 and photographs/text actually read. One nest found 8 April in herbaceous cover within cork-oak forest; three eggs and a chick photographed on 23 April. V3 selects a later 29–35-day early fledgling with developed soft folded wings, short visible tail and firm low support while retaining mesoptile head/body signals. BirdLife 2025's current monotypic treatment conflicts with older named-form sources; the plate uses species-level southern African identity and explicitly excludes direct transfer from the Moroccan record. Exact individual age, first-flight threshold, continuous feather sequence and universal sex ratio remain unknown; science false.
- Related: [research note](species/78-asio-capensis-v3.md) · [v2](../design/concepts/78-asio-capensis-v2.png) · [run](../production/runs/20261029T1036+0800-cycle-1.md).


## 2026-10-29 — #79 *Otus lettia* v3 — 11–15-day cavity-nestling feather boundary

- **LET-LEADPRATHOM-2009 (new, primary):** Kumron Leadprathom, V. Chimchome & S. Bumrungsri, “Nesting Ecology of the Collared Scops Owl *Otus lettia* in Thailand”, *Ardea* 97(4):457–461 (2009), DOI [10.5253/078.097.0409](https://doi.org/10.5253/078.097.0409), BioOne PDF https://bioone.org/journals/ardea/volume-97/issue-4/078.097.0409/Nesting-Ecology-of-the-Collared-Scops-Owl-Otus-lettia-in/10.5253/078.097.0409.pdf ; accessed 2026-10-29. Indexed full-PDF text and Kasetsart University repository record actually read; printed p.459 “Chick growth and development” reports: hatchlings closed-eyed in natal down with egg tooth; 6–10 d remiges and semi-plumes begin, eyes open on average 6 ± 2.8 d; 11–15 d bill bristles begin; rectrices do not begin until 16–20 d; 21–25 d are fully feathered/fledged. The same study observed 23 nests in Chanthaburi, mostly upward-facing tree cavities, nestling period 18–26 d (n=6). High for species-specific chronology and nest substrate; one Thai study, not a universal individual schedule. BioOne’s interactive PDF was security-blocked, so the readable indexed PDF text plus university bibliographic/abstract record is retained rather than claiming page-image inspection.
- **LET-HKBWS-2023 (new, institutional regional account):** R. W. Lewthwaite & G. J. Carey, “Collared Scops Owl *Otus lettia*”, *The Avifauna of Hong Kong* v1.0 (2023; page updated 10 Jan 2024), https://avifauna.hkbws.org.hk/species/0190/027100 ; accessed 2026-10-29. Identification/breeding/systematics sections and references actually read: 23–25 cm; large reddish-brown eyes, pale yellowish bill, prominent tufts, pale/grey black-rimmed facial disc, brown/buff/black mottled upperparts and streaked/vermiculated pale underparts; juveniles noticeably paler; common nest sites include tree holes/stumps/boxes; page treats *O. lettia* as polytypic. High for Hong Kong adult identity/current regional treatment and nest-site diversity; does not sex adults or give early feather chronology.
- **Reused:** SUN-BSS-CURRENT (Bird Society of Singapore: current local *O. lettia*, dark eyes/reddish orbital ring, small conspicuous tufts, pale hindneck collar, brown vermiculated upperparts and sandy-buff fine-streaked underparts; juveniles paler/fine-barred); WHSO-MIKKOLA-2013 printed p.162/PDF p.164 (historical photographic-guide baseline; no usable sexed measurement ratio).
- **Correction/application:** v1–v2 retain their older pale fine-barred perched juvenile and common-baseline adult scale correction. V3 deliberately selects the earlier 11–15-day cavity stage: open eyes, pale natal/early semiplume body, bill bristles beginning, short developing remiges but no rectrices yet. It must be belly/sternum-supported on a dry cavity floor, not standing, climbing or flying. Adults remain near-equal scale because the sources read do not establish a reliable sex-size direction. Exact day of eye opening for an individual, pin-feather count, subspecies transfer and sex ratio remain unknown; science false.

## 2026-10-29 — #80 *Otus sunia* v3 — 7–10-day pin-feather cavity stage

- **SUNIA-PUKINSKY-1977 (new, species-specific field synthesis; secondary transcription):** Yu. B. Pukinsky, *The Life of Owls* (Russian field monograph; original 1970s field material), digitized chapter “Scops owls” https://zoomet.ru/puk/puknski_OTUS.html ; indexed text actually read 2026-10-29 (direct open returned 403). The chapter attributes 2–3-day chick descriptions to V. A. Nechaev and later nest observations to V. M. Polivanov: pure white dense down; brownish juvenile iris; by nights 6–7 chicks sit supported on the tarsi and can move; by ~2 weeks greyish cross-barred mesoptile; at 3 weeks they first appear at the cavity entrance. It also reports 7–10-day chicks with remige sheaths just beginning to open and nest departure around 25–28 days. Medium evidence for chronological design boundaries, but this is a digitized secondary synthesis without verified page images; do not treat every individual or population as exact.
- **SUNIA-NO-DISS-2017 (new, primary thesis metadata/summary):** Sun-Ho No, “Studies on ecological characteristics and conservation plan of Oriental Scops Owl (*Otus sunia*) in South Korea”, doctoral dissertation, Kongju National University (2017), National Assembly Library record https://dl.nanet.go.kr/detail/KDMT1201763633 and RISS record https://www.riss.kr/search/detail/DetailView.do?control_no=2ab76a52b9b7d809ffe0bdc3ef48d419 ; accessed 2026-10-29. Table of contents and English summary actually read: chick growth section printed pp.114–115 / Fig.19; hatch mean wing 15.8 ± 0.8 mm, tail 9.1 ± 0.5 mm, mass 9.1 ± 0.3 g; pre-fledging wing 95.8 ± 10.8 mm, tail 36.0 ± 4.0 mm, mass 58.4 ± 6.5 g; mean nestling period 23.1 ± 2.1 d. High for Korean growth endpoints and timing, but accessible summary does not expose daily plumage photos.
- **SUNIA-NO-2015 (reused, primary):** No et al., “A study on breeding ecology and nest characteristics of Oriental Scops Owl (*O. s. stictonotus*) in South Korea”, *J. Ecol. Environ.* 38(4):415–424, DOI 10.5141/ecoenv.2015.044, OAK full PDF https://oak.go.kr/repository/journal/21148/STHHCL_2015_v38n4_415.pdf ; printed pp.415–418 and methods/results actually read. Thirteen nests: woodpecker holes 46.1%, natural tree holes 38.5%, boxes 15.4%; mean brooding/nestling period 23.5 ± 2.6 d; fledging defined as stable flight to tree top; cavity space affects wing exercise. High for Korean nest substrate/flight definition, not daily feather colour.
- **Reused:** WHSO-MIKKOLA-2013 printed pp.152–153/PDF pp.154–155 for adult yellow eyes, dark bill, small tufts, grey-brown morph and fluffy faintly barred mesoptile. Female mass difference is not converted into a fixed height ratio.
- **Correction/application:** v1–v2 retain the late, fluffy, weakly barred nestling. V3 selects 7–10 d: white/cream natal down still dominant, brownish-not-yet-yellow juvenile iris, a few remige sheaths just opening, no developed rectrix fan; low tarsus-supported cavity-floor sit/shuffle is allowed, but no upright branch perch, cavity-entrance display or flight. Exact day-by-day tract sequence, population transfer and sex ratio remain unknown; science false.

## 2026-10-29 — #81 *Otus scops* v3 — approximately 21-day fresh-fledgling boundary

- **SCOPS-MARCHESI-SERGIO-2005 (new, primary field study):** Luigi Marchesi & Fabrizio Sergio, “Distribution, density, diet and productivity of the Scops Owl *Otus scops* in the Italian Alps”, *Ibis* 147:176–187, DOI [10.1111/j.1474-919X.2004.00388.x](https://doi.org/10.1111/j.1474-919X.2004.00388.x), full Wiley HTML actually read 2026-10-29. Methods on pp.178–180 report nests checked around 15–20 d and published fledging range 20–32 d; ≤7-d hatch dates were backdated from feather development. Most local laying nests were holes/cracks in buildings (95%, n=20); low-elevation agro-pastoral habitat. High for regional nest timing/habitat, not an individual plumage series.
- **SCOPS-GRIECO-2025 (new, primary post-fledging study):** Fabrizio Grieco, “Early movements of Eurasian Scops Owl *Otus scops* fledglings and the response of territorial neighbours”, *Ardea* 113:103–115 (2025), DOI [10.5253/arde.2025.a6](https://doi.org/10.5253/arde.2025.a6); abstract/full-text index actually read 2026-10-29, plus its preceding 2023 bioRxiv methods index. Italian Aleppo-pine study: young leave at about 21 d, move progressively away on following nights and remain parent-fed to ~40 d; in this cavity-poor site they used old Magpie twig nests. High for fresh-fledgling age/movement and support context; indexed text did not expose a calibrated feather photograph.
- **Reused:** SCOPS-BLASCO-UNDATED ringing manual pp.1–3 (sexes alike; juvenile fresh flight/tail feathers are narrower and pointed, adult feathers broader/rounded; juvenile photo 09-VII previously viewed); SCOPS-OWLPAGES-2020 and WHSO-MIKKOLA-2013 printed pp.122–123/PDF pp.124–125 for yellow eyes, grey bill, cryptic grey/brown morph and residual down direction.
- **Correction/application:** v1–v2 retain the formed-wing climbing juvenile and scale correction. V3 assigns the stage to an approximately 21-day fresh fledgling: fully supported low Aleppo-pine branch beside an old twig nest, narrow pointed fresh juvenile remiges/rectrices, residual fluffy down on crown/flanks, short controlled step/hop but no sustained flight pose. Adults near-equal; morph is not sex. Exact individual departure day, flight competence, feather width and sex ratio remain unknown; science false.

## 2026-10-29 — #82 *Otus senegalensis* v3 — 25–28-day fresh-fledgling boundary

- **SEN-RIEKERT-1986 (new, primary):** B. R. Riekert, “Some observations on the breeding biology of the African Scops Owl”, *Madoqua* 14(4):425–428 (1986), DOI [10.10520/AJA10115498_480](https://doi.org/10.10520/AJA10115498_480), [Environmental Information Service Namibia record](https://the-eis.com/elibrary/search/8960). Full four-page PDF actually opened and all printed pp.425–428 visually inspected on 2026-10-29. The article reports artificial-nest-box observations and presents growth/development material for six chicks. High for species-specific nest-box and development context; the small rendered pages do not justify an exact daily feather-tract map.
- **SEN-BROWN-RIEKERT-MORSBACH-1987 (corrected/verified primary anchor):** C. J. Brown, B. R. Riekert & R. J. Morsbach, “The breeding biology of the African Scops Owl”, *Ostrich* 58(2):58–64 (1987), DOI [10.1080/00306525.1987.9634144](https://doi.org/10.1080/00306525.1987.9634144), [EIS Namibia record](https://the-eis.com/elibrary/search/15071). The institutional record and publisher abstract were actually read on 2026-10-29: two pairs in nest boxes at Daan Viljoen Game Park, incubation about 22 ± 2 d, nestling period 25–28 d, female incubates/broods. This upgrades the old search-only lead `SEN-BROWN1987-UNVERIFIED`; abstract-level access does not expose a calibrated plumage series or first-flight threshold.
- **Reused:** `SEN-BE-ROBERTS2005` (natural cavities/arid savanna; departure 25–28 d at roughly 75% adult size); `SEN-OWLPAGES-2020` (adult diagnostics; females normally heavier; young leave at 3–4 weeks and acquire adult plumage later); `SEN-MIK2013-130` printed pp.130–131/PDF pp.132–133 (yellow eyes, blackish-horn bill, feathered tarsi/dusky toes, shoulder band, sex-mass and morph cautions).
- **Design application:** V3 selects a recent fledgling at 25–28 d, about 75% adult size, making a fully supported low branch-step beside a nest box/tree cavity in arid Mopane/Acacia savanna. Retain soft grey-white mesoptile/residual down, fresh soft-edged folded wings and a short tail; prohibit adult-regular white shoulder/spot rows, hard layered mature remiges and sustained-flight pose. Adults use the same grey-brown morph and near-equal skeletal height; female mass is not converted to height.
- **Unknown/limits:** exact feather-tract geometry at 25–28 d, individual first-flight competence, subspecies transfer and juvenile sex remain unknown. Concept remains scientifically unverified.

## 2026-10-29 — #83 *Otus brucei* v3 — approximately 26-day fresh fledgling at date-palm cavity

- **BRU-BENDOV-KIAT2016 (reused and access-corrected primary field source):** Amir Ben Dov & Yosef Kiat, “Breeding Pallid Scops Owls in Rift valley, Israel, in spring 2015”, *Dutch Birding* 38:146–158 (2016), [official issue PDF](https://www.dutchbirding.nl/journal/pdf/DB_2016_38_3.pdf) and [author full text](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/305657832_Breeding_Pallid_Scops_Owls_in_Rift_valley_Israel_in_spring_2015). Full searchable text was actually read again on 2026-10-29. Printed pp.151–152: all nests were in cavities of mature date palms, 2–3 m below the canopy; fledging occurs at about 26 d. Printed pp.156–157/plate 243: nestling/fledgling comparison supports pale coloration, deep-yellow iris, pale eyebrow, horizontal forehead/chest/belly lines and greater middle-toe feathering. High for this Israeli first-fledgling/habitat boundary; small regional sample and no exact daily feather sequence or sex-size ratio.
- **BRU-QATAR-ENATURE-2026 (new, institutional secondary identification account):** Qatar e-Nature, “Owl, Pallid Scops”, [species account](https://www.enature.qa/specie/owl-pallid-scops/), actually opened 2026-10-29, description lines 38–61. Supports current name *Otus brucei*, pale sandy-grey adult, sharply demarcated dark streaks and completely barred juvenile underparts. Medium for regional identification; it gives no nest age, sex ratio or feather-development sequence.
- **BRU-IOC-15.2 (new, current taxonomy framework):** Gill, Donsker & Rasmussen (eds), IOC World Bird List v15.2 (2026), [official site](https://www.worldbirdnames.org/), actually opened 2026-10-29; version/citation lines 141–152. Repository retains the species as *Otus brucei*; no historical or subspecies assignment is forced from this general list.
- **Reused:** `BRU-MIK2013-126`, printed pp.126–127/PDF pp.128–129, for adult pale iris/dark grey-horn bill/fine crown shaft streaks, limited tarsal feathering and the caution that female mass is not a fixed height ratio.
- **Design application:** V3 uses a fresh fledgling at approximately 26 d on a broad date-palm cavity lip: about three-quarters adult scale, pale sandy-grey woollier contour, deep-yellow iris, pale eyebrow, horizontal forehead/chest/belly bars, middle-toe feather patch, fresh soft-edged folded wing and short tail, both feet supported. No basket, adult-clone wing, sustained flight or forced subspecies label.
- **Unknown/limits:** exact age of the photographed individual, daily feather-tract geometry, first-flight competence, adult sex-size direction and Israeli subspecies remain unknown. Science false.

## 2026-10-29 — #85 *Otus semitorques* v3 — first-winter retained flight-feather geometry

- **SEM-FUKAI2017 (reused and access-corrected primary anchor):** Norio Fukai, “Age determination of the Collared Scops Owl *Otus lempiji*”, *Bulletin of the Japanese Bird Banding Association* 29:25–31 (2017), [official J-STAGE PDF](https://www.jstage.jst.go.jp/article/jbba/29/1/29_MS086/_pdf). Full abstract and paper text were actually read again on 2026-10-29. Printed pp.25–27 and Fig.1: Tochigi birds historically named *O. lempiji semitorques* map to current *O. semitorques*; juveniles retain narrow, pointed remiges/rectrices plus pale short primary coverts, adults have broad rounded flight feathers plus dark long coverts. These juvenile feathers are retained through the first autumn until complete molt the following summer. High for the first-winter diagnostic, but based on four young and one recaptured adult; iris colour was not recorded and must not be used as the age classifier.
- **SEM-ANTIPIN2014 (new primary breeding observation):** M. A. Antipin, “Notes on the breeding of the Japanese Scops-owl (*Otus bakkamoena semitorques*) on Shikotan, the Southern Kuril Islands”, *Amurian Zoological Journal* 6(2):211 (2014), DOI [10.33910/1999-4079-2014-6-2-211](https://doi.org/10.33910/1999-4079-2014-6-2-211). Printed p.211 and color plate VIII were actually read 2026-10-29: three downy chicks of unknown age were found 22 May 2013 in a large artificial plastic drum nest. This confirms a white/downy nestling stage but provides no age series; it is deliberately not transferred to the selected first-winter design.
- **SEM-TOKYOZOO-2026 (new institutional life-history cross-check):** Tokyo Zoological Park Society, “Japanese Scops-owl / オオコノハズク”, [Inokashira species account](https://www.tokyo-zoo.net/inokashira/encyclopedia/japanese-scops-owl/index.html), actually opened 2026-10-29, lines 147–165. Current name *Otus semitorques*, forest/tree-cavity use, May–July breeding, and white-down hatchlings. Useful for current-name and habitat/stage separation; no sex-size or first-winter feather details.
- **Reused:** `SEM-MIK2013-158`, Mikkola printed pp.158–159/PDF pp.160–161, for nominate adult red-orange iris, grey-horn bill, pale brows/double nuchal collar, herringbone underparts and toe feathering. It supplies no quantified sexual dimorphism.
- **Design application:** V3 depicts a supported first-winter juvenile near adult scale in early-autumn Honshu woodland, with narrow paler pointed retained remiges/rectrices and shorter paler primary coverts; adults show broader darker round-tipped flight feathers and longer darker coverts. No natal down, chick proportions, flight pose, bare-toed Okinawan transfer or iris-based age claim. Adults remain near-equal in skeletal height.
- **Unknown/limits:** exact month/individual age, sex of the young, quantitative feather-width/color thresholds, first-winter iris, and adult sex-size direction remain unknown. Scientifically verified remains false.
